LADIES' LETTER.

JAY DEAR NELLIE, Blouses are to be much worn again, but not in severe contrast with the skirts. Thiu season the blouse is to tone with the jape; for instance a fawn skirt would be accompanied with a fawn silk blouse, a navy blue skirt with a navy blue and white stripe or check blouse, ezc., while smart in- deed are the black coats and skirts with white glacd silk or satin blouse. The new evening skirts; when elaborately trimmed and the frou!frou reaching on the underlining nearly to the kciee, are effective and pretty, otherwise they may trans- form the wearer into a perfect caricature. The new skirt is certainly that which, being quite flat at the back, fastens at the side or front in a scalloped line. The great art is to have those skirts cut so that they flare at the bottom. Many dressmakers are ignorant of this; they make the line of mitres high enough to admit of a shaped flounce, which results in dwaifing a small woman to a most unbecoming extent. The triple tunic is a preparation for the flounced skirt of the summer, and a very pretty jupe is made with throe-shape folds at the hem. These take the place of fisiunces or bias folds, as they borrow from the style Of both. When a lace yoke is adopted, it is smarter to have the top of the sleeves to match, and as most of these yokes are only a pretext for wearing white next to the face, they are usually made o: snowy satin or glace, with a covering of creamy lace or guipure.

Some very seasonable skirts are made of checked tweed in shades of drab and white, with a narrow line of blue running through, and the coat is made of plain fawn-coloured cloth, with facings of the cbeck material on the cuffs and collar. The but- tons also are made of the check material. The coat is suitable for wearing with shirts or blouses, and sometimes with a vast of fawn cloth of a paler shade than the coat. Another costume in blue cloth is trimmed with black mohair braid at the edge, which is cut out in the fashionable scallops, and it is fastened in front with buttons, arranged in sets of three. Some very stylish cycling hats and skirts have became fashionable just now, and one skirt parti- cularly has been brought out this season, which will, no doubt, commend itself to anyone who likes comfort combined with smartness. This open's at the back, but the placket hole is ingeniously arranged to fall under a very, deep pleat, and it cannot possibly gape or become visible. The pocket is in front, but a good deal lower down

than it. has been customary to place them, and the result i 3 that the contents do not make an an- sightly protuberance on the hip. At the back the skirt is tying in an inverted box pleat, so that it looks well either for walking or riding, and when on the machine the pleats fall gracefully on either side of the saddle. The skirt alone can be made of smart and serviceable materials very cheaply. Floral toques intermixed with loops of wired tulle are the latest whim of the fashionable modiste but tulle is by no means confined to toques, and many of the large hats have folds and bows which look too ethereal with groups of ostrich plumes. Flowers also adorn the big shapes of fancy straw, and these are in huge clusters, hydrangeas, lilac, pansies, and roses, the latter in weird, unnatural colourings. Nasturtiums are much affected in black hats and toques, and possess so many shad- ings that they are most accommodating, and can be worn with various gowns. There is also a smart toque of white horsehair, embroidered with steel sequins, mauve silk, and silver tinsel threads. The crown is formed of green ivy leaves and shaded mauve roses, and on the left there are flat bows of mauve glace, caught in the centre with round paste buttons. This toque can also be made in any other colour.

I have noticed, too, soma exceedingly nice nautical costume for girls of tine blue serge, ornamented with silver braid. You will have seen, too, perhaps, a smart and new walking- dress of spotted foulard in a shot blue and green effect. It is trimmed with narrow bands of peacock-blue ribbon velvet, and at the shoulders, waist, and neck there is some thick creamy guipure lace, mounted on white satin. With this is worn a hat of Tuscan straw, trimmed with innumerable loops of ribbon velvet, and a large cluster of pink roses on the left side. Your loving

quite enormous, and having a smaller stone above and below it. An emerald is set between each two opa's, and b ordering the bracelet are some fine brilliants. So far pare ads have not proved of very much use,and the greater no mber remain in their dainty boxes, the shop windows, and show rooms. The very latot are extremely light in colour, and should be carefully avoided unless the purchaser is able to indulge in a variety, and so escape being known as the woman with thb pink parasol, the blue parasol, or the violet parasol, as the case may be. Those for festive occasions are literall y covered with frills of chiffon, the more costly with embroidered bills. The- pink of the almond blossom is the favoutite lining, and this appears with almost every colour. For young girls wear, the sailor dress has put in an appearcnre again, which is a fart to rejoice over, as the style is so pretty, so simple to make, and put on when made. The sailor blouse too is much worn again, and as it is a delightfully easily garment to manufacture at home, should commend itself to those gies whose dress allowance is limited. Two or three vests of different colours can bl built with each blouse, and with a band to match round the sailor hat, and neat cuff frills, and waist belt en quite, changes can be rung very economi- cally.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

The Duchess of York grows very like her mother, the late Duchess of Teel. She is gaining confidence, and that mask of coldnese,which really was assumed to cover nervousness, is fast dissppearing. A more sunny,happy disposition it would be difficult to find, but the Duchess I is perhaps seen at her best in her nursery, surrounded by her little ones. She i passionately attached to them and like the Duchess of Fife, spends all her spare hours in " nurseryland." She is a great favourite with her sisters-in-law the Duchess of Fife Princess Charles of Denmark, and Princess Victoria of Wales, and with her mother-in-law, the Princess of Wales. Princess Christian has had a very busy time lately, and until the Princess of Wales' period of mourning is over she is not likely to have much time at her disposal. Her Royal Highness bowever,is always bright and cheer- ful, with a kindly smile of recognition, or a pl,astnt word for everyone. No one his ever seen her out of temper, or angry. In her home Princess Christian has always governed her numerous dependents by love, which is the ruling sentiment of her existence. Her children are devoted to her, and regard borne as the happiest place. Cumberland Lodge, althrugh a Royal residence; can- not be termed a' stately English. home. It is just a picturesque, old-world looking mansion, whose ivy-clad walls convey a sense of intnnal comfort more than of state. The hall contains some very fine pictures. ard sporting trophies are here in profusion, for Prince Chris- tian is a keen sportsman.lThe dining room is a large hand- some apartment, and here again many paintings form the decorations of the walls. From the windows, a view of the adjacent country is seen, with a green velvet lawn in the foreground. Away in the distance' a glimpse of a lake is caught, as the sun rests on its calm surfacer and the gentle waving of the trees beyond gives a sylvan aspect to the view.

The Princess's boudoir is quite unlike a modern woman's dainty useless apartment. A fine toned p'ano speaks of her musical abitities which are exceptionally brilliant, a work basket proclaims her industry, a busi- ness-looking writing table tells tales of hard work, books scattered here and there show that the Princess keeps herself au courant with tbe literature at the day, to a well-filled ease of serious books denotes her wish and refresh herself with the thoughts of departed geniuses. A beautiful harp occupies one corner of this delightful room.

Of all musical instruments, the harp is the most picturesque, its grateful triangular outline, and curvel neolc vieldint:ra beauty independent of decoration. The rage for the instrument early in the present cegtury was quite phenomenal.gf Every girl was expected to learn the harp, regardless of sore fingers or natural distaste. But the greater effectiveness, and facility of the pianoforte as it improved, drove the harp out of the drawing- room, and it is only within the last year or so that it has re-appeared there. The harp undoubtedly has great advantages of its own. Finely tapered fingers, a flexible wrist, a shapely arm, a graceful figure.and a small foet. may he displayed without blame, for the necessities ot the inFtrument require it. Great sympathy is felt for Lady Herbert Naylor-Ley- . .

land, in the loss of her husband, -whose devoted nurse and companion she has been during his long wearying illness. Hyde Park House.where Sir Herbert died is one of the most splendid of mansions. It is that huge square house at Albert Gate,at the opposite corner to the French Embassy. It has therefore the enormous advantage of looking to Hyde Park, one of the finest prospects in London. Lady Herbert has been little seen about for some time past, but when taking her shnrt daily drive, it was evident to all that her husband's illness was weighing heavily upon her. Her little boy, who is a godson of the Prince of Walee,occasionally accvapanied her. _ _ .

It is doubtful if any man living has been godfather to so many children as the Prince of Wales. He has now rffi- c,ated in this capacity about eighty times. His Royal Highness makes it a rule too, never to lose sight of his gcdchi/dren. Suitable gifts are forthcoming from time to time, and he invariably evinces the keeneqt interest in their career. No other woman in the world probably poss' 8555 so many godchildren as the Empress• Eugenie. On the day the Prince Imperial was born she exnresaed herself willing to act as godmother to all the children born in France on that day. These numbered 38,34! Golden-haired. pink cheeked beauty has quite gone out, and the fashionable woman of to-day cultivates a pale cemplesion, dark hair, and a pair of very red lips. There is one item in favour of the dark hair, and that is, dyes with which to effect this, are leas dan7erons, generally speaking, than those bright red. or !Hat tints demand. Entire wigs are very een.ral, the majority of

society women posse.sing three or four. Those are kept " dresseo "ready to be slipped on id a moment, and so beautifully are they manufactured that eetecticn is al- most an impossibility. The fashion of wearing the hair reached perhaps its highest pirch of absurdity during the latter half of the eighteenth century, when the custom of raising it alsove the head was introduced from France. The erections worn at first were comparatively moderate in sizo but they rapidly increased in bulk and extent, until a fully fashionable head-dress towered quite three feet above the crown of the brad. As the hair had to be plaster-4i together by means of a mixture of pomatum and meal, and stretched over a mass of tower wool, the discomfort of the vieqrer can be better imagined than described. Before 1786 the size of the headdress had begun to abate I considerably, and a year or two later a new fashion of wearing the hair in long cnrls'had.come into vogue.

Dyeing the hair began with the Roman dames, as early as Cato the Censer, when reddish yellow was the " only wear," a colour obtained frorn the use of caustic soap imported from Gaul. Later on other colours nre veiled, each in turn. Ribbons, pins, threads of go'd 00, d- or strings of pearls, wreaths, and carved combs, were regular parts of the toilette, and Psts of special nornatums, kpt closely secret, are preserved. The Emma. Plotina had five and twenty. Flexible bracelets are still in high favour, ornamented all round with gems, torquoiFes or opals preferable, always with a setting of diamonds. The best of these are so arranged that they may be clasped together, two doing duty as a necklace. The Priecess of Wales is very fond of bracelets, and usually wears several, two in Particular rarely leaving her arm. One, the gift of the late Princess Alice is of gold set with diamonds and tnrquoises, bearing on an oval framed in single tur- quoises, the cypher "L.A.", and a crown of brilliants on a ground of rock crystal. The other is composed of opals, diamonds and emeralds. The opals are arranged in a row, and graduate in size, the central one being

A MODERN ROMANCE.

BY JOHN BLOUNDELLE-BURTON, Author of "Fortune's my Foe," "The Scourge of God, "Across the Salt Seas," "The Clash of Arms," "In the Day of Adversity," "A Gentleman Adventurer," "His Own Enemy," "Denounced," Ito.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Arabella Waldron. a young girl, is to sail for India in a ship belonging to her uncle, Captain Pooley, in order to be married at Bombay. But, previously, the reader is to be told of her courtship. A year before, the Captain had taken her to the theatre, where she had met Stephen Charke, a young friend of her uncle, who is smitten by her. Afterwards, at a lunch at Portsmouth, Bella meets Gilbert Bampfyld, a flag-lieutenant to the Rear-Admiral, and at a ball in the evening she gives him most of her dances. After starting for Bombay to marry Gilbert, who is stationed on a warship there, a cable comes for Bella not to start, as the warship has been ordered to Africa. She is, however, already out of reach. But, on the way to India, the ship meets with a slave dhow, and on that dhow, mysteriously wounded, is Gilbert Bavnpfyld.

CHAPTER IX. "Come over, come home Through the salt sea foam."

Never, perhaps, on all that old highway of the waters, that silent road along 'which so many had steered their course to fabled Ormuz and to Ind, nor amidst fierce sea-fights 'twixt Arab and Persian, or Arab and European in later centuries, nor in the howl of storm when the waters closed around the shipwrecked and doomed, had there arisen a more piercing shriek than that which now issued from Bella Waldron's blanched lips. "My God!" she screamed, repeating the second mate's words. " The name is Bampfyld! ' Oh! it's Gilbert—Gilbert. There is no other in the Navy list. Let me go to him, uncle!" "No, no," the master said, while good Mrs. Pooley put her arms round the girl as she stood there by the poop-rails and endeavoured to calm and soothe her. "No, no, Bella; they will bring him on board directly, then "—and in his desire to ease the girl's heart he raised the ghost of a smile to his lips—"then you shall nurse him till he is well. Yet," he muttered to Charke, as he walked over to where the first mate stood, "yet, how on earth does he find himself in that infernal dhow!" "Heaven knows," the other answered. "But, t, perhaps, 'tis not so strange, after all. There may have been a fight between his cruiser and the slaver —though there's not much fight in them when they get a sight of the British Flag!—or he may have been sent to board her and got cut down, or half -a- hundred things. All of which," he added, with his now usual cynicism," are equally likely or unlikely. Anyhow, he is here—or will be in a few moments, and we shall have him for a pas- senger to Bombay. Your niece is in luck, sir," and he turned on his heel and went down the ladder to the deck to see to the nosing of the boat, which was now making its way back to the ship. To Stephen Cheri:6, still loving the girl as madly as he did, still raging inwardly at the knowledge that every knot which the ship made was bringing her nearer to the -man who, as he considered, had torn her from him, this incident seemed the last and most crushing blow of all. God knows what hopes the mate had cherished in his bosom since first he had learnt that Bella was to be a passenger in her uncle's ship for about four months; what ideas might have been revolving in his mind as to whether, in those four months, something extraordinary, something almost unheard of—not to be dreamt of nor foreseen—might happen to give him one more chance of winning her. He was a romantically-minded man, a man with so rich an imagination that, to him, there came sometimes ideas that few are ever burdened with. And, in that full and teeming imagination, there had been pictured to him visions of the Emperor of the Moon being wrecked and Bella and he alone spared—he, of course, saving her at the peril of his own life—and winning her away from her more aristocratic lover by so doing. Or, he dreamt of that lover being himself wrecked and .lost, or pierced by an Arab spear in some affray, or shot in a hand-to-hand fight with a particularly bold slaver (though all the time he 'knew well enough that the slavers had no fight in them!) or—or—or—he cherished any mad vision that first rose to his brain. And now—now—this very man, this successful rival, this aristocratic naval officer with his high birth and future peerage was actually being brought aboard the ship where the woman was whom they both loved—brought on board "slightly wounded, and his own last chance thus gone. Gone for ever now! Perhaps, therefore, it was no wonder he should bite his lip and smother unholy murmurs deep down in his throat; perhaps, too, he merits compassion. He had loved this girl fondly since first he had set eyes on her, and once, at one time, he thought he had almost won her. Then this other had come in h way, had swept him out of Bella's heart, or the approach to it, and his chance was over. Yet, once again, they had met through an almost unheard of, and scarcely to be imagined opportunity, and—lol here was his successful rival once more at hand to thwart him. It was hard on him, or, as he muttered to himself, "deuced rough." The quarter-boat was coming back to the ship now, Fagg steering her, while, between him and the stroke oar, there lay, the body of the young naval officer, clad in his "whites." And again as Bella, madly whispering "Gilbert, Gilbert, my darling," stood by the head of the accommodation ladder—which had been lowered while the boat was gone to the dhow—the men brought her lover gently up and laid him On the deck under the awning. "Oh Gilbert!" she cried again, as now she bent over him, stroking his hair which, on the left side of his head, was all matted with thick coagulated blood, "Oh Gilbert! to think that we should meet thus! Sir!" she screamed to Fagg, who was about to descend again to the boat to fetch off the others still in the dhow, "where is he wounded? Where? Have you had time to discover ? " "I have looked him over, Miss Waldron, and, to tell you the truth, I do not think there is much the matter with him." "Thank God! Oh, thank God!" "That blood," the second mate continued, "comes from a heavy contusion at the side of his head, but the skull is uninjured. Also there is no concussion—observe the pupils of the eyes are not at all dilated." Then he turned away and went swiftly down the ladder again, muttering that if he was to save the Arabs and the negroes there was no time to be wasted. The dhow was filling fast, he added. There was a big hole in 'her below the water-line, and a quarter of an hour would see the end of her. And now Gilbert was carried to the cabin corre- sponding with Belle's on the port side of the vessel, aft of the saloon, and Mrs. Pooley, with the steward, went in to undress him, telling Bella that as soon as he was comfortably placed in the bunk she should come and take her place by his side. Whereon the girl, distracted by both her hopes and fears—hopes that the second mate was right in his surmises as to her lover's wounds, and fears that he was wrong—sat herself down on the great locker that was in the gangway, and gave herself up to tearful meditations. "Ah 1 if he should die!" she murmured; "if he should die! Then my heart will break." Though, as you shall see, and have undoubtedly divined, Gilbert was not to die then, at least. But by this time other things were taking place • above, which were almost as startling as the dis- covery of Lieutenant Bampfyld in that slave dhow. Startling, not only because of the unexplained cause that had brought the Arab slaver into this portion of the Indian Ocean, but also because of the strange and mysterious behaviour of those others who were now being conveyed on board the Emperor of the Moon. Ere they came, however, Mr. Fagg had sent over information surprising enough in itself, and sufficient to prepare all on board the ship for what, a little later, they were to see. "The owner—if he is the owner," he cried; "the man who fell down while steering, is dead. He is stiffening. I presume I had best leave him to go down with the dhow ?" "Aye," called back Pooley. "Aye. the others? " "I cannot make them out. The negress seems well enough, but terribly frightened. As for the four men below, they all appear blind. We have taken their shackles off and they grope their way about as though in the dark. My men have to lead them up, the ladders—yet their eyes look clear enough." "Have they been kept in the dark think you, and is the sun dazzling them now ? " "No. sir. The open zratings have furnished the

What about

part of the hold they were in with ptenty or clot.- I .Ele paused a moment, and those in the Emperor saw him gazing down steadfastly to that hold; then he called again: "We must come away now, sir. The Water is pouring in. The dhow will not swim much longer. • "Do so," answered back Pooley; and five minutes afterwards the boat was on her way to the ship, laden with the rescued negroes. And Mr. Fagg had proved right in his surmise as to the necessity for lerYing the slaver at once. Ere they came on board, she was observed to heel over a little to starboard, then to further do so with a jerk, then, suddenly, she righted until she was on a level keel —and, next, sank suddenly like a stone, the body of the dead man who had fallen down while steering &one remaining above the heaving waters, and being swirled round and round in the whirlpool caused by the wreck, until it too went down. Creeping up the companion, their hands directed to the side ropes by the sailors; feeling the steps with their huge splay feet—as a mule feels its way along the thin line of insecure path that rounds the smocth face of a precipice—those stricken men came; huge, splendid specimens of the swart negrocis of Wyassa and Wahiyou and Wagindo, whom the Arab slaver ships from Kilwa, below Zanzibar, and transports to Bussorah and Mohamra, whence they often reach the more distant Turkish harems. Now, looking at them as they stood on the deck of the Emperor of the Moon, it seemed as though their course was almost run. For, though these men would never be slaves to Arab or Persian or Turk, of what use to himself or anyone else is an unhappy 'blind nigger who, to exist at all, must work like a dray-horse? "Poor wretches!" said Pooley to Charke as they both stood regarding these blacks, "see how the shackles have eaten into their ankles. Poor brutes! They say that sometimes these fellows sell them- selves willingly into slavery; I doubt much if these have done so. Ah, well! we must take them with us to Bombay, where at least they will be free. I wish," he addet "we could communicate with them somehow—learn who and what they are." "Perhaps," said Charke quietly, unemotionally as ever, "Lieutenant Bampfyld can tell us when he comes to. Since he was in the dhow, he probably knows what she was and where she came from.' Then, breaking off to cast his eye around, he said: "Sir, there is a breeze coming aft. Shall we not make sail? " - "Aye," cried Pooley, springing to the poop, "aye, we have had little enough wind for some days. Summon the watch." A few moments later the order to square the yards thundered along the deck; the men rushed to the braces. Far off, up from the dusky wizard south, the wind was coming as they fisted the canvas, and the Emperor, heeling over, gathered way and sped once more towards India. Gathered way faster and faster as outer jib and topsails were loosed and sheeted home, fore and main topgallant sails and spanker yards braced sharp up, and mainsail,r mainroyal and 7,mizzen topgallant sails set, as well as jibs and staysails. She talked, as the sailors say, as she went through the water; she hummed and sung beneath the breeze that came up from far down by the Antarctic circle—a breeze whose cool breath was gone, and was instead'per- fumed now by the warm spicy odours of .Mauritius and Reunion. • Away. over the vast waste of golden waters she flew, and the master, standine, on the poop, called down to his first mate joyfully to ask him if this would not do well enough for Bombay. "Aye, aye, sir," answered Charke, turning round from giving brders to the men aloft .to answer his chief. "Aye, aye, sir." While to himself he muttered: "Bombay! India! Well, when we are there, all is ended for me!"

CHAPTER X.

THE GROWING TERROR.

The sailor is, as all the world knows, a light- hearted, mercurial creature. Face to face with death in some form or other during every hour of his life—although often the mere presence of death is neither known nor suspected—he is only too elated and happy when momentarily without any- thing to cause him anxiety. Such was the case now with Captain Pooley, since his beautiful ship was rapidly picking up 1111 the time she had lost by lack of wind and delays, "and since," as he jovially phrased it, "all his new passengers were doing well." "First and foremost," he said that evening, as he sat at the head of his table with Bella in the place of honour on his right hand, his wife on the left, and Mr. Fagg opposite to him, while his honest, sunburnt face gleamed rubicund beneath the inch of white under his hair which his cap had preserved from the sun, "Bella's young man is all right. Then Bella's new plaything, the tiger, takes kindly to its lodgings-rthough you will have to sell it, my child, directly we get to Bombay, and distribute the money it fetches among the men. Then those wretched slaves—even they will eat and drink, won't they, Fagg ? " "Not much .of that, sir," the second mate answered, who was eating and drinking pretty well himself, however; "they don't care to do much of either. They make a good deal of moaning up in that deck cabin forward which you have given them. The woman, however, seems all right. I suppose the lieutenant has not been able to tell you much about the dhow yet, Miss Waldron ? " he asked, bending forward a little as he addressed her. "At present," she replied, "we" have had little conversation. He says, however, that he sent me a telegram from Bombay, telling me not to start as the Briseus was coming down here. He only came to an hour ago, however," she went on, while a ravishing blush swept over her face, "and we—we. —have had so much to—" "Spare her, Fagg," said Pooley, with a laugh, and passing the claret at the same time. "Spare her. Suppose you woke up one fine afternoon and found your sweetheart bending over you in your berth and whispering all sorts of endearing things in your ears, as well as kiss—" "Uncle!" shrieked Bella, while Mrs. Pooley touched her husband's arm reprovingly with her forefinger, and Mr. Fagg hid his face behind the vase of brilliant Cape gooseberries on the table. "Uncle!" whereon the bluff, good-natured sailor desisted, and began to speculate on the blindness with which the rescued negroes were attacked, and on that attack being, as he imagined, a recent one. "They capture these poor wretches inland," he went on musingly, "in the big lake region as often as not; but, as fs,r as I have ever heard, blindness is not one of their afflictions. Moreover, these Arab owners and captains wouldn't buy blind slaves, either for selling further north, or for using as sailors in their dhows. Therefore, I take it, this blindness must have come on them since they were shipped. That's strange, isn't it ? " and as he spoke he rose and went to his neat mahogany bookcase, which was securely fastened to one of the saloon's bulkheads, and took down the two medical books which he possessed—the one dealing with all general human complaints to which our flesh is heir, and the other more especially with tropical diseases. Yet neither under the heading of "Eye," nor "Blindness," nor "Optics, " could he find aught that bore upon the subject; nor in his book on tropical complaints could he discover any informa- tion that might enlighten him as to why the four negroes should be so stricken. He spoke again, however, after turning over the leaves of these erudite volumes a second time, say- ing: "Fever, I know, sometimes produces blindness as an after effect, yet—well, we have all seen these fellows, and there's no fever in them, I should say. Oh, deuce take this confusion of tongues!" he exclaimed irritably; "if it did not exist we could find out so much from the sufferers themselves. Bella, our only hope is in you and your patient. , If Lieutenant Bampfyld can't toll us something, r we shall never know who these men and the woman ' are, where they came from, what is the matter with them, and to whom the dhow belonged. Can he speak anything but English, child?" "He knows some Hindustani," Bella replied, ; "and, I think he said, some words of Swahili. He has taken up Eastern languages in the Service, which was one of the reasons for his being appointed to the Briseus. He may be some help. At least he can tell us how he came on board that horrid ship." As she spoke eight-bells struck on deck, and, as the sound came through the skylight, both she and Mr. Fagg rose, the _girl doing so because it was the hour at which she intended to visit Gilbert again, and the latter because it was time to relieve Stephen Charke, who would now come below to take his supper. Also. Bella had fixed this hour for paying her last evening visit to her future husband, because she knew that the second mate would then descend, and she was never now desirous of being more in his company than necessary. She therefore left the saloon before Fagg could have relieved Charke, and, going to the cabin in which Gilbert 13ampfyld lay, pushed back the cur- tain that hung at the door and went in to him, observing as she did so that he was awake and lying gazing upwards. Also she saw that he smiled

nappuy on perceiving her, and whispered the word, "Darling" as she advanced to his bedside. "You are better, dearest," she said, bending ovei him and putting her hand on his forehead, whick as cool and moist. "Much better. Aunt will anima sonn with fresh bandages for vonr poor head. and then you will have a good night's refreshing sleep. And to-morrow, perhaps, you will be able Le tell us how you came to be in that hideous slaver. Oh Bertie! "—for so she often called him-- "what a mercy it was that we found you as we did. A.nd what a miracle that we should have met thus. Home keeping and narrow-minded people would say if they read it all in a book that such a thing was unnatural and impossible." Their first meeting, their joy at discovering that they had come together again in this marvellous manner; their rapture when, a few hours before, Gilbert Bampfyld had emerged from his stupor and unconsciousness, has not been forgotten, although the description of it has been omitted. Omitte& for the simple reason that most of us have been, or are, lovers; most of us have known in our time, or know now—and those are the happy ones !—the sweet, unutterable joy with which such meetings are welcomed. Who does not remember the sudden, quickened beat of the heart at some period of their existence, when they were once more face to face with those they loved the best of all in this world; the creature upon whom their thoughts were for ever dwelling, and from whom these thoughts, however wandering they had heretofore been, had learnt at last never to roam. Picture to yourself, ' therefore, what rhapsody was Della's when, for- getting everything else but that she held her lover to her breast, she wept over his salvation from an awful, swift, impending death; picture also to yourself the delirious joy which coursed through Gilbert's unclouded mind, as he found himself in her arms—with her—close to her. Picture this, and no further description is needed of their meet- ing in that cool, darkened cabin of the old ship. Imagine for yourself what your own ecstasy would have been in such or kindred circumstances, and you possess the knowledge of what theirs was. "Darling," he said again now, as she held to his lips a cooling drink that she had brought into the cabin with her, "darling, I can tell you in half a dozen sentences or less—"

---- ------__ __ _ "No," she said. "No; not now. To-morrow, when you have slept—" "Yes, now. Why, dearest, lam well! I could take the middle watch to-night if necessary, or—or —do anything that a sailor may be called on to do. And as for finding me in that dhow, why! it's the simplest thing on earth—or the waters. t Listen. The Briseus, as you would have learnt by that telegram I sent you if you had ever received it, was suddenly ordered to join the Cape Squadron—dhow catching. And I can tell' you we i were not so very long before the game began, since by the time we were abreast of Kilwa—which is the southern limit of the legal slave trade--we fell in with twelve dhows, one of which was our friend from which I was rescued out of by your people, And you may depend we were after them like •lightning, beginning to ply them with shell and shot from our little gun forward. They scattered, of course, though some got hit and lay disabled on the water, while I went off in the whaler with her crew to attack one that seemed badly knocked about. The one in which I was when found by you." - "The horror!" exclaimed Bella, with a pretty shudder. "No, no; don't call her that, because, after all, I owe my life to her." "The angel!" exclaimed Bella now, with sudden change. "Though I don't altogether know that the captain meant,to save me—" . "The wretch! I'm glad he's dead." Gilbert laughed at these variations in Bella 's , mental temperature. Then he continued: "They are artful incredibly artful these dhows. They will let our pinnaces or whalers, or , any other of the ship's boats come alongside—then s , all of a sudden they cut their lee halliards, and ! down comes their great sail over us, enveloping , the boat and all in it, just as if it was in a net or bag." "Ah!" gasped Bella. !I "And that's not all. When you are caught like that they have another pleasing little way of firing at you from above and through the canvas, so that you are being shot down while, all the time, you have no chance of escape." "Oh I " exclaimed Bella. "There! Ah, th* wretches." "They do, Bella. Fortunately, however, this one couldn't do it, being disabled, and she had, therefore, come up to the wind with hardly any way on her. This was all right for us, as I meant to board, so, as we came alongside each other we hooked on to her anchor cable which was hanging pretty low down, and we should have got on board too, only at that moment the dhow gave a lurch which sent the whaler half-seas under and I got a blow on the head that knocked me insensible— " "Oh Gilbert. That wound on your head!" "I suppose so. At any rate, I knew no more about it; and I don't know anything further either, since, till I woke up here this morning and found you bending over me, I had no recollection of anything. It's a wonder the negoda didn't kill me. Perhaps, however, he was already ill or wounded. He seemed a little jumpy when we got alongside." "But how did you come into the dhow you were found in ? " Bella asked now, and pouring out, directly afterwards, one question after another. "And when did it happen—yesterday or a week , ago? And where was the whaler, and the sailors ' and the Briseus? And why did they all desert you? What a nice kind of a captain yours must be, to be sure!" "My opinion is now," said Gilbert, "that the dhow you found me in rescued me, picked ma up. Also, I expect our captain—he is a rattling good skipper, Bella, all the same—heard I wall; drowned and thinks I've missed my muster. My; Cousin Jack w 1 imagine for a month or so—till we get in to Bombay—that he is the future Lord D'Abernon," and he laughed as he thought of hov, soon Cousin Jack would be undeceived. "But the dhow we found you in—how did she escape, and why didn't the Briseus capture her ? " "Some must have got off in the confusion, also it was only an hour from dark. I'm certain to be reported lost when the ship goes into either Zanzi- bar or Aden, and— What's that ? " he ex.- claimed, breaking off suddenly. "Surely that's your uncle's voice?" He recognised it because Captain Pooley bad been in to see him -after he recovered his con- sciousness and had congratulated him on doing so and being pris,„ically restored, as well as saying that he was delighted at being the means of rescuing him out of the sinking slaver. "Yea," Bella replied, "that's uncle's voice; and the other is that of Mr. Charke, the second mate." "Listen! What is it he is saying ? " It was perfectly easy to hear what he was saying, since both master and mate were conversing in the saloon, to which Charke had descended. And the words which reached their ears, as they fell froio the latter's lips, were: "Oh! no doubt about it whatever, sir; not the least. The nearess is now' as blind as the negroes themselves. She cannot see Iher way along the deck, nor any of the signs WS have made before her eyes." (To be continued.)

The small diagram will enable our eaders to understand the notation by which the moves of the game are recorded. Black occupies the squares num- bered 1 to 12 and white those numbered 21 to 32. The figures thus (11-15) Indicate that a move is made from and to the squares so numbered. Black has the opening move and the pairs of figures apply alternately to Black and White to the close.

WINWECK COUNTY ASYLUYL SOME INTER ST [P PIRTICULA.RS.

It is a sad fact that the necessity for the new asylum at Winwick is so urgent. Speaking gener- ally, the great need for increased accommodation Is due, we suppose, to the growth of population in Lancashire, but if-- and we do not say it is so—the increase in lunacy follows in proportion to that of the pop ulation, then it necessarily follows that the causes that tend to lunacy are still strongly existent and find their full percentage of victims. No unprejudiced person can deny that strong drink is the most a ctive ageet in this respect. Magistrates, poor law administrators, and ministers of the gospel are continually testifying to this—and there are few people who do not fully realise it. But besides the harmful effects of over-indulgence in alcoholic liquors on the nervous system, there is the ever-incr easing strain and worry of business life adding its quota to the number of persons whose mental balance is more or less impaired. Cempeti- tion is becoming keener and keener, and the inevi- table result is that the weakest competitors "go to the wall "—or to the asylum. These are sad facts, but being facts tney have to be considered, though it is not onr intention to deal further with them in this column, which is intended to give the people of Warrington a fuller acquaintance with the progress of the extensive building operations at Winwick, and the purposes for which they are required when complete. The site is an admirable one, and from it one can get a splendid view of the surrounding country. Every effort is being made to preserve the fine old trees on the grounds and for the purpose of preserv- ing a magnificent beech which was regarded as the monarch of the park, the plans were subjected to alteration. The old hall, at the present time, is being utilized as an asylum for boys and there are about 50 patients under the charge of Mr. and Mrs. Mitchell, toe master and matron. The extent of the building operations is impressive seen from the road, but one realises it more completely when directly on the spot. The writer, searching for Mr. Beaman, the clerk of the works—to whose courtesy we are indebted for our information—found the utmost difficulty in finding him even with the assistance of such a competent guide as Mr. Ormiston, assistant clerk of the works, so heterogeneoue and complicated does the mass of briek-work seem to a stranger. However, all things have their end and after some trouble which was not al; ogether unprofleable,Mr. Beaman was run to earth whilst personally superin- tending some particular work going on. Probably one or two facts will better serve to convey to the minds of our readers the amount of work to be done in the erection of tais institution. The work has now been going on for about two years, and will, in all probability continue for two and a half years more. Bricklayers to the number of 200 are en- gaged, joiners 90, navvies 150, and the rest make up a total of 800 actively employed. The total cost of the buildings is estimated at £300,000 of which £14,000 will be expeoded on the installation of elec- tric light. The buildings with the air courts will stand on 22 acres of land on a bed of Rainhill stone 56 feet in depth. The asylum will be a full sized one,and will accommodate 2,000 petients,the largest number of inmates allowed in one institution by the Lunacy Commissioners of this country. Messrs. Henry Crisp and Oatley and Mr. W. S. Skinner of Bristol, are the joint architects for the work. Messrs. R. Neill and Sons. of Strangeways, Manche stet, are the contractors for the work, and the sub-contract for brickwork,roads, and drainage has been let to Messrs Cis vvortn and Robinson, of, Manchester. The brick-yard in connection with the place is an extensive affair itself, and is under the direct superinte udeuce of Mr. Beaman, who has telephonic connection bet ween the yard and his office. Sixteen million bricks have so far been laid including glazed, buff, ruabon and fire brick, and of this number IA million bricks have been turned out on the spot by Messrs. Batley and Hodges, of Burton-on-Trent, and the rest have been supplied by the contractors. One can thus understand that brick-making is a very impor- tant detail of the whole, and the brickyard, judged by its output. will bear comparison with the plant and resources of many wholesale bricirmakers in the country. The institution will be in eighteen blocks. The chimney stack now completed is 170 feet high, and the water tower nearly completed will be 150 feet high. Eight steel boilers will also be necessary, three to run the electric light—the installation plant of which is on a very extensive and improved scale—and five for the work of the laundry and the efficient maintenance of ventila- tion and heating. The blocks will include one for the superintendent, and separate blocks for the nurses, chaplain, ticspital,steward,clerk,clerk of the works, engineer,attendants, block,isolation hospital, two lodges, administration department, workshop, engineers' yard, electric installation plant, etc. A. very striking feature will be a splendid recreation hall which is now in an advanced state and will be 120 feet by 60 feet in dimensions, exclusive of stage space and dressing rooms etc. The official entrance block will be of three storeys. The buildings will be mostly of two or three storeys in height and naturally for such a huge place,the storage of water to be equal to the requirements must be oil a large scale. The measurements of the rain water tank will be 106 ft. by 50 ft. by 10 ft., and it is being con- structed on the most approved principle. A Gothic church, which promises to be an exceed- ingly pretty erection,is to be built and will accomo- date about 850 people. The air courts are very spact- o-ae and the,corridors will be well lighted. The de- sign of the whole is of the letter H shape,and there will be two miles run of roofing in the buildings valeta complete. Toe huge dining-room will be 38 ft high from floor to ceiling and there are also very exten- sive Intchens,serving rooms,waiting rooms.and visit- ing rooms. The main corridor will be about 121 t wide and the large official block will be approached by a new road—already made—:rom Winwick-road. Water is being supplied from the Corporation Water Works, at Winwick, and an eight inch main has been laid to the works. There are about 4 mortar mills working day and night on the premises of which Mr. Bennett has the superintendence. The mortar used in connection with the building is composed of Llhysfeten lime and hard engine clinkers. Cement floors are being laid by Messrs Ilindley and Co., of Manchester, and Tingey and Sons' well-known cement is being used. khe plasterers' work has been much delayed owing to the lock-out, and rase Monday week the :seasons went out for shorter hours and tel per hour more money, which would make their wages 90, instead of 811. It was their desire to start at 7 o'clock in a morning, and leave off at 5 o'clock each night. A settlement has been come to late this week, with a concession of Ld per hour the men to start at seven instead of six o'clock in the morning. The inside of the centre blocks is being lined with glazed bricks, and nearly all the blocks and corridors are to be fitted with fire proof ceilings. Constable Sharp is living :in one of the lodges and is therefore at hand in case of necessity, and Mr. Beaman himself is staying in Hollands-lane Lodge. The work is being carried out in an expeditious manner, and the fact that comparatively few accidents have occurred reflects great credit on the management and the supervi- sion. Probably some particulars in reference to those who are responsible for the satisfactory carry- ing out of the labour in the different Jepartments will not be unacceptable. The stone and woodwork is being erected by Messrs. Ngeill, and fire proof floors are being hid by Messrs. 13Ennett and Inglas of London. Mr. Robinson is the general foreman, Mr. T. Roberts, of Newton, Mr. Richardson and gr. Jump are the working foremen of the joiners, and Messrs Finch, Peacock and Williams foremen of the bricklayers. To continue the list we may say the manager of the brickyerd is Mr. Ormrod ; Mr. Jeffries, of Manchester, is doing the plumbing, and the plastering is in the hands of Mr. Evans, of Manchester. Mr. Colthurst is foreman of the masons, and the engineering work is entrusted to Messrs. Haden and Sons, Trowbridge. Mr. Robert Curran, architect of Warrington, has the laying of the railway under his control, the drain pipes have been supplied by Messrs. Edwards of Ruabon. and the laundry machinery and fittings by Messrs. Bradford of Manchester. Mr. J. A. Ewart, of Warrington, we may tide, has completed the erection of 12 cottages and two lodges. The completion of the asylum will be a matter of con- siderable importance to Warrington and the sur- rounding districts in many respects, and we feel quite justified in an anticipation of this kind. The institution will be a solid looking structure quite in keeping witb the character of the town which will be most affected by its erection.

TFIE DRINK QUESTION,

Mr. A. H. CR;;;;ELD-attea le I at the Cemetery Hotel on Saturday evening for the purpose of playing a nine of billiards, 250 up, with Mr. Arthur Jones. There was a good attendance, and Mr. Crosfield, who is president of the Bowling Green Club, had a very hearty reception. The table, whice is an excellent one, has been used for some weeks, but the match on Saturday evening was re- garded as a formal opening. There were no big breaks, Mr. Crosfield's best effort only preducine 27, but there were some very pretty and art:stic shots in it. He also scored 23 and 18, 17 b•ing the hiehest numb3r totalled by his opponent, who had very " hard lines," the balls running very badly. The result was Mr. Crosfield, 250 ; Mr. Jones, 146 ; the former thus winning by 101 points. Mr. SAMUEL TAYLOR, in propo3ing a vote of thanks to Mr. Crosfield for his attendance, said he was a great supperrer of ell kinds of sport, and the members of the Cemetery H otel Bowling Club were much obliged to him for acting as their president. They hoped he would stick to them until they gave him the "sack." (Laugh- ter.) Mr. Jenne WILLETT, (who bad marked and called the game) seconded the motion, and observed that since Mr. Crosfield had been p-esident of the club it had made rapid strides. They had had the pleasure of winning the championship of the Lancasaire and Cheshire County, arid the second team won the second team championship. Last year they were in the final, but, unfortunately, they" went down," and the only explanation which could be found was that their men were" played out." They knew what it was to play and play until they be- came stale: Had the final been pieced three weeks earlier they would have come off with flying colours. , (Applause.) Mr. Crosfield did not stand alone in his connection with the club so fir as Messrs. Crosfield's works were concerned, since they had Mr. John Crosfield, IDr. Markel and Mr. Welsby as vice-presidents. (At). plause.) The motion was agreed to with applause. Mr. CRO3FIELD, in reply said he was extremely obliged to Mr. Taylor for the very kind manner in which he had proposed the vote of thanks, which was somewhat superfluous. He certainly could not attempt to rival Mr. Willett's eloquence, because he had just had to un- dergo the severe ordeal of tackling a first-clase player. He thought however. he had done more than his share en other occasions in the matter of making speeches. (Hear, hear.) With regard to the match it had been his turn tbat night ; it would be Mr. Jones's turn next time. In the &it place he wished to congretulat3 Mr. Allison upon having built the room, and upon the excellent table he had provided for the com- fort and convenience of the members. He had dis- played an enterprise that he very much admired. It had struck him in thinking about the matter that if their good friend the Bishop of Chester were to come to Warrington and Pe9 that establishment, and the lines upon which the club wag run—respectable and Leigh class lines—he would admit that it weat a very long way to solve the problem he had so much a; hear% And when the time came fora selection to be made he hoped they might see in these times of the " eurviv al of the fittest" other establishments of thatfkind for med—ex- callent clubs in the best sense of the word—for the bene- fit of the townspeople. (Applause.) The billiard room made the premises and that interesting cub pretty well complete. He was sorry that neither Mr. Jones nor him-elf were in a very destructive mood that night. He was told that his record stood at 43, and perhaps on another oecasion they might manage to break it. He had not been breaking anything this evening. He supposed they were in a very cons rvativa frame of mind. (Laughter.) Allusion had been made to the forthcoming bowling contests. They had shown very good form in the club during the past few years, and he hoped all the bowlers would buckle to and do their best this season. (Hear, hear.) It had been a pleasure to him te be associated with the club, and he accepted their invitation to continue as their president a little longer. (Applause.)

BY "OBSERVER."

This question is at the present moment claiming a large share of public attention and its importance is only realized by those who have fully considered it. Owing to the action of municipal authorities who are petitioning Parliament in large numbers in favour of it, the subject has assumed a different aspect altogether. It, is no longer a personal one between the lessor and the lessee. _ _

As some misapprehension exists as to what is meant by ground values the following simple state- ment may help your readers to better grasp it. By ground values we understand the value of land over and above the fair rental obtainable for it as agricultural land. Land is a commodity that differs essentially from all others for by no process can it be multiplied or iecreased. How it comes to be in so few hands has often been the subject of enquiry and there are not a few in the country who contend that much is held on insufficient titles. It is undeniable that large tracts of land are held by single owners whose ancestors had it bestowed upon them subject to certain services. At this day all such services have ceased to be paid or rendered and the growth of the population has added enormously to the capital value of such lands independently alto- nether of the special value they possess as building land. It will be easily apprehended that land within and adjacent to a town and which is being appropriated for building purposes fetches a price from ten to one hundred times in excess of its value as agricultural land. Now the owners efland within the building zone knowing full well that there is but a certain quantity make no secret of their intention to get all they can. In addition to the rent asked they compel their lesee to submit to all sorts of onerous conditions and invariably they compel him to covenant to pay all rates and taxes and other im- pesitions except Landlord's Property Tax and there is little doubt the lessee would have to pay that were it not for a provision in the Income Tax Act which enables the tenant to stop it from his j landlord and the landlord dare not refuse to allow it under a heavy penalty. There are even landlords who make their agricultural tenants pay that true landlords' tax the Land Tax. It is also well-known that until recently, land—the most valuable of all our securities was also free from Probate duty and it needs no special know- ledge to guess how this came about. The land- owners were not likely to tax themselves. This Sparttan like sense of duty does not seem to rise spontaneously in the British landowner's breast, The present Parliament has given us a good illustra- tion of how the landowners can look after them- selves. It frequently happens that a landowner having let on building lease portions of a field, can afford to permit the remainder to be uncultivated, and until his own price is reached he will refuse to to let at all. He realizes full well that every dwell- ing put up reduces the quantity remaining unbuilt upon and that as a consequence if he wait long enough he can get his price. Illustrations of this action are on every hand. Not only does the land- owner hold out for his price but he will not un- frequmtly introduce conditions in his leases which make it utterly impossible for business to be carried on profitably thereunderjand as a result localities have greatly suffered. Many of the olct inhabitants of Warrington can no doubt recollect the time when various important works were projected and would have been erected here but for the inability of the promoters to get suitable:land. It is not necessary to go so far back however for rumour has it teat the proprietors of one of our existing important works would have added many acres to their present take, and that a large firm from the midlands would have taken another plot near to them but for the difficulties and stipulations imposed by the proposed lessor. Further—how many overcrowded streets in busy towns are there which are overcrowded simply be- cause the owner of the only land available will not sell or let land at all for workmen's dwellings ? Here, we are told,suffering and sickness are abound- ing and the medical officers and the authorities are powerless. surely the ownership of land carries with it some obligation to the authority by whose acts and expenditure the benefit and increase of value comes to such owner. Too often is all this quite overlooked—the land is mine, may I not do what I will with my oyes, in effect, says he. Let us consider for a moment what is done by the land- owner himself. Ile lends his land to a man or a company who erects thereon a property upon which property the landowner has a first charge for his ground rent. If a street is to be cut through the land naturally we would imagine the landowner would open up the street and make and pave it. Well, they do not do that sort of thing in Warrington. Mainly, if not entirely, the land for ' the street is measured in to the leaseholders and they have to pay the same rent for the land forming the street as for the land upon which they have built their property. Then afterwards, when the street has to be formed, channelled and paved, the lessee has again to pay all the cost. Agricultural land becomes building land mainly owing to the advantages offered by the district—suitability for works, railway and canal conveniences. The gather- ing together of numbers of people compels the authorities to expend large sums in public improvements such as widening thoroughfares, gas works, water works, public parks and gardens, public libraries, expenses in sewer- fog and sewage works, sanitary works, street cleansing, &-.e. All this expenditure is helping to enrich the landowner, by adding to the value of his land still unbuilt upon, and yet we do not find that in any way whatever, does he help or contribute to this expense. Another question arises. Does not the whole of this really come out of the pocket of the man who occupies the property ? The lessor demands his price per yard per annum for the land. The lessee then builds, and puts to the ground rent the interer., upon the outlay in buildings, and the rates he has to pay, and thereupon he fixes the rent the tenant must pay. All this clearly points to the conclusion that one man only, and that one the man least able to bear it, is bearing a burden that should be shared by the wealthy landowner who may, and does, under the existing lawasave himself from the payment of his rightful share. Practical suggestions are now being made to re- medy this and as Parliament has sanctioned the principle in the case of the Tower Bridge improve- ments there is no reason why it should not go further. It is proposed that the land upon which premises are built should be rated by itself and that the buildings also should be rated by them- selves. That the owner whoever he may be should pay the assessment on the land and the occupier of the premises should pay the assessment on the buildings. This is surely simple enough ! It may be argued that this of itself would not bring any additional revenue and that it is only shifting the burden of the rates but it would be found to do so in many places. Ofttimes the land is let at a certain ground rent and a cash bonus; at others it is let on short lease and an additional ground rent or a fine 13 charged on renewal. These sums are considerable in some places and at present bring in nothing whatever to the rates, for not being known they thus escape. It is from the land however that is not let that the great benefit would be derived. Let this be included in the assessment and we should soon feel the benefit of it. How unjust,' fancy I hear some one say, to put a rate on land that is not productive. Is it ? Just think for a momentewhere is there anything more productive? The price per yard is continually going up ; it in- creases by leaps and bounds. It is certainly not productive of an annual revenue in the ordinary sense but when the annual value rises from say ..E:2 10s. per acre per annum, to £4O and even up to -£240 an acre per annum then the product is evi- dent enough. If it were rated at its letting value the landowner would not care to go on paying the rate very long. He would speedily become more reasonable in his demands, nay, he might even go out of his way to induce lessees to come and take it from him so as to escape the burden. Then it would be no injury to him. The natural value of the laud to him is its value for agricultural pur- poses and if he elect to forego that and let it at from ten to one hundred times that value he is only con- tributing to the community some slight acknow- ledgement for what the community has done for, him. Another result, and a very important one too is that there would be a reasonable prospect of those who occupy the premises living at a reduced rental so that the benefit would be an all round one. The cry for this reform has long been uttered to deaf ears, but now appeals of a new character are being made, and appeals that will not be withstood. The grievance is a personal one between the Lessee and the Lessor but between the Lessor and the com- munity which shelters him,nurses his land for him, puts value into it while he sleepe,is a national one end when this demand for equitable treatment is pushed home it will be with a force that cannot be withstood.

SILVER STREET WESLEYIN SUNDAY SCHOOL

Very successful anniversary services were held in Bewtey-road Wesleyan Chapel on Sunday in connection with Silver-street Sunday School. Once again the Rev. F. C. Haime, supernumery of Bristol, was the preacher. In fact, this anniversary has been inseparably connected with Mr. Haime's name since he was a minister in the Warrington circuit over twenty years ago. The children from the Sunday school were accommodated on an im- provised platform in front of the rostrum, and they very sweetly sang a number of hymns. Mr. B. Wadsworth, the organist, bad taken great pains in the training of the ceildren, and their tinging was highly creditable. At the moraine, service, the anthem "0 praise God" (Rev E. V. Hall) was sung by the choir. Mr. Povey presided at the afterneon service, and in the course of a few remarks he expressed his high apprecia- tion of Sunday School work. He said it was impossible to over-estimate all the influences the Sunday schcol might have upon the lives of the rung people gathered in it. Even out of a school like that there might arise future town councillors, and even some boy present might be the future Mayor of the Borough. An address was delivered by the Rev. G. Flarbottle, who spoke on the beautiful gate of the t,mple. Religion, he pointed out, had an important influence even upon the life pre- sent, and as they believed, determined the future destiny of each one. There were many gates into what they might call the temple of life. Some entered it at the close. There was a gate near the end of life ; the mercy of God saved peopie who had not until the close of life given themselves to His service. Some again, entered upon a religious life in middle-life. They had tried everything and found satisfaction in none and at last found rest in Christ. Many, as the Chairman had said, left the Sunday school thinking they were to@ big for it but when they grew to manhood, in their early manhood they gave themselves to God. But what might be called a beautiful gate of the temple was thae which the Sunday school held open to those who in their early youth under the guidance of Sunday school teachers and friends gave themselves te God and entered the beauti- ful gate and their entire life was a life spent in the ser- vice of God and for the good of mankind. Of such they might say that evon death itself became a beautiful gate int) Eternal life. The choir sang two anthems, and Mr. G. Glugh contributed a ' solo entitled " Eternal Rest " (Piccolomini). At the evening service, the church was filled to overflowing. , The preacher based his remarks on the text "Who is my , neighbour ?" St. Luke 10c, 29e. He vividly drew the picture presented in the chapter, of Christ teaching the lawyer how to attain eternal life, and to take everyone for his neighbour that needeth his mercy. He wished them to realise the fact that everyone was their neigh- bout, and that it was their duty as Christians to let the spirit of kindness run through their lives. In the anthem" Conquering Kings their titles take," Mrs. Stone was the seloiet. She also sang in the anthem " Jesu, priceless treasure," in which Mr. Baker and Mr. Corbett also to-,k part. The collections for the day realised the satisfactory sum of £42 Hs 2d.

THE ROCK IN THE ROAD.

In the middle of the road between two country vii - !ages wag a big stone, firmly set in the earth. Like an iceberg in the sea thera was more of the stone under the ground than ab we it. Colts grew horses, boys grew nsen,and beards grew grey; yet that stone stayed in the middle of the road. Horses stumbled os er it and whe•As rubbed against it year after year. Mee cursed it for being there, and said it ought to be removed. They said it wore out horse shoes and waggon tyres enough to pay the cost of its removal a thousand times over and so it did. The road was occasionally banked with more diit by the people who used it, but they never bothered with that rock. Perhaps they might tackle it next season, they said.

And S 3 the big stone held its own for a generation onger. Then r eturning to my native place, and passing along that road, I missed the familiar and ancient rock. What had become of it? Why, this. A gentleman of push and brains had come from the great city and settled on a farm near by. He was one of the men who do things. Driving out from the station the night of his arrival, his carriage struok the venerable impediment. He got out and 100 ked at it. The next day a man came and drilled a hole in it, A hour later there was a dull explosion, and the horse laming, wheel-wracking, tem- per-trying old nuisance w as seen to more. Gracious me! How many applications there are of this less= from the Primer of living. Here is one : A %valuta, Hrs. Susan G rover, of Hertfordshire, had suffered from indigestion and liver complaint for thirty years. She habitually felt drowsy, tired, and weary, as if carrying a weight. She had, so she says, frequent attacks of gidd mess, and was liable to fall on any spot where they might overtake her. 0 n her tongue, sa valuable a member of the human

body, was a constant coating of fur,which had no honest business to be there. It spoiled her taste, and what was worse it—but let us stick to our facts : the explanation can wait.

Her appetite on the whole was hardly worth calling an appetite. S ometiEnes she could eat with a sort of feeble relish, and then she and her meals met as persons divi- ded by an old grudge. The victuals looked good, and they were good, but not good for her. Still one must eat a little anyhow, whether he wants to or not; for nature ordains it. So Mrs. Grover did. But she knew what would happen—pain in the chest and in Jur right side—Who re the liver is. Her rest was not what it should have been for health and happiness. It was broken,and struck through with bad dreams,and she felt the effects of it in the morning. This made work go hard, tut she did her best, and got along; yet it was difficult. Labour without the rewards of labour—that's the cross which was laid on the poor woman's shoulders. She had a prevailing sense of sick ness, and often brought up a bitter fluid—a mixture of bile and other secretions. And with all this there was Chronic costiveness of the bowels. & bad and vile aggre- gation of disorders had ed. " For thirty years." says Mrs. Glover in her letter, "I lived in this way—sometimes a bit better and then down in the low grounds again. What I went through I could not set forth in words if I tried. I took all kinds of medicines that I got to hear of; and had medi- dines from the doctns over and over; but no help came of them. "I was finally cured eby Mother Seigel's Syrup. A neighbour. Mn. Johnson, first told me of it. She said Stv ea her life. I began with it, and in only a few days it relieved M 3 as one would not think

plsiibls. I could eat with a relish, and with- out pain. I began to feel light and bright, as though I had entered a new couatry and a new air:

Briefly, after a few more bottles of Mother Seigel's I found myself well and strong. My life-time ailment had been removed,and the wonder was how easy and quickly this medicine did what the others could not do at all You may print my letter and refer to me." (Signed) SUSAN GROVER, 24, Solesbria.e-lane, Chorley Wood. Rickmangworth, Harts, o.3tober 21, 1898.

A. WARRINGTON MAN'S FOOLHARDY

BET. FATAL RESULT.-THE INQUEST.

Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest on Tuesday afternoon, at the Police Parade Room, War- rington, on the body cf Mark M`Gann, aged 36, of 17, Dale-street, Sankey Bridges, who was drowned in the Mersey, near Atherton's Quay, Warrington, on Saturday night.—Mr. A. Browne appeared on behalf of Mr. F. Turner, landlord of the Coach and Horses, Liverpool- road, and the Chief Constable watched the proceedings on behalf of the police. Mary Hayes, 17, Dale-stre3t. Sankey Bridges said the deceased had lodged with her for about :six weeks. He was a forgo labeirer. She last saw him alive on Saturday between 6.30 and 7in the evening. He was sober.—By the Chief Constable : She had known deceased about eight years. He had the reputation of being an expert swimmer.—ln answer to the Coroner, the Chief Constable said it was knows to the police that deceased could swim with his hands and feet "ded.—cross examined by Mr. Browne wit- ness said deceased Came from his work about ten minutes to six on Saturday afternoon. Henry Thompson, 118, Liverpool-road, said he was in the Coach and Horses with deceased who came in about ten minutes to eight. He had one glass of beer to drink and then said he would bet anyone " six pints " he could swim across;the river Mersey. Deceased put down is. and witness put 13. down also, and someone held the money. Deceased was Bober and witness and several others went out with him to the riverside. At about eight: c'clock he dived into the river, which was at low water, and' when he had gone about 50 feet—nearly three parts across—he suddenly disappeared, evidently having bean seized with cramp. He never came up again, and a man obtained a boat and went to the spot but could nr.t see him. By the Chief Constable: He had heard of deceased jumping in the canal and swimming from Buttermilk Bridge to Sankey Bridges for a pint of beer. John Dunbobbin, in charge of a boat with a dredger, said he discovered the body in one of the buckets on Monday night. He thought from the appearance of the bedy that deceased had been seized with cramp. In answer to the Coroner, Thompson said McGann was not swimming out of a drunken bravado. Mr. Fairfield Turner, licensee of the Coach and Heves, stated that he had known McGann for eighteen years. He was only served with one glass of bser at his house on Saturday night. He was sober, and only stopped in the house about ten minutes. Witness knew hisn to be an eepert swimmer. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death."

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

ERSKINE-FANSHAVii.

The wedding took place on Tuesday afternoon, in Sr. ' Thomas's Church, Stockton Heath, of Mr. Herbert H. Erskine (formerly of Belfast, and now of Johannesburg, ind son of the late Mr. H.M. Erskine.of Belfast) to Miss Maud Fanshaw, third daughter of Mr. D. Fanshaw, of Whitecross House, Warrington. The bride who was given away by her father, looked very pretty in a grey dress, trimmed with white chiffon and white moire, and she carried a beautiful shower bouquet, composed of white roses and maidenhair fern. Her bridesmaids were Miss Madge Erskiue (sister of the bridegroom) and Mies Beatrice Fanshaw (sister of the bride), and they wore dresses of white lustre and white picture hats trimmed , with ostrich plumes. Each carried a choice bouquet of white lilies. The vicar of the parish. the Rev. H. V. Pigot. M.A., officiated, and the best man was Mr. James McCullough, of Belfast. Mrs. Fanshaw, carried a very i charming bouquet of pink roses and lilies of valley. Mr. Ernest Wilkes, L.L.C.M., presided at the organ, and contributed an excellent selection of music before, during, and after the ceremony, including "Adagio Molto " (Dussek). Fantasia by Tours. Allegro from "Sonata " (Dr. A. L. Pearce) • " Wedding March" (Mendelssohn). On the return to Whitecrose House, the guests sat down to an excellent repast, the health of the bride and bridegroom and other toasts be- ing submitted and suitably responded to. Later in the lafternoon, with the best wishes and congratulations of their friends, the happy couple lef; for a tour in North Wales, the bride wearing a blue costume. It is expected that Mr. and Mrs. Erskine will sail in the Pembroke Castle on the 18th inst. A large number of weful and handsome presents have been received, and on Tuesday afternoon several congratulatory telegrams were received at Whitecross House.

WARRING/ON UNION ASSESSMENT COMMITTEE.

A meeting of this committee was held on Friday in the Board-room at the Workhouse. There were present : Messrs G Pilling, A J Jolley, A Jolley, J Billinge, J Clarke, S Davies, William Houghton, T Halton, William Hickman, with Mr J C Sutton (clerk) and Mr 8 Sutton (assistant clerk).

===== 0002601_18990512_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF THB CHAIRMAN.

Mr. DAVIES moved the re-election of Mr. George PiHint:: as chairman, and remarked that whoever occhpied the chair could not have done so more efficiently and dili- gently than Mr. Filling had done.—Mr Clarke seconded.— Mr Hickman supported and said a gentleman observed to him the other day that he had been before the Assess ment Committee twice. Ths first time he was snubbed and the second time he was treated like a gentleman. Mr. Billing° said he had been congratulated upon the continued increase of the assessmonta of the Warrington Union. There were persons in other Unions win) were bemoaning the loss of assessments.—Mr A J Jolley advc- cated the sticking to a good man when they bad got one and net changing for the sake of change.—Mr Houghton and Mr Halton supported and the resolution was carried. —Mr Pilling thanked the members for the renewal el their confidence and said he looked upon the Assess- , meat Committee as the leading committee of the Union.

THE VICE-CE-fAIRMAN. Mr, WILLIAM HOUGHTON proposed the re-election of Mr. A J Jolley as vice•c'aairman and said that next t , Mr. Pilling himself they c)uld not find a oetter man f.,r the position.—Mr. HICKMAN seconded and the resolutiou was unanimously carried. TUE VALUER'S REPORT.

Roberts and Mr. Edwin Brad- shaw, who wore subtenants to Mr. Parkinson. Messrs H and S Chandler, Egypt-sr., £75, £64. Joseph Crosfield and Sons Limited, additions to works wharf and warehouse, and for "The Great Central Hotel " as directed by the committee. 'The Mersey White Lead Company : On inspection ho found alterations were being made in respect of the erection of a chimney which would cause other, alterations in the buildings about it. The offices had been improved and the house which was occupied and separately assessed had been thrown into the works. As the alterations in connection with the chimney were only now proceeding, he had proposed to leave the matter over until a full valuation could be made. W. D. Houghton, Sankey Wire Mills: Plans had been prepared for the valuation of the additions in these works and he would bring forward the assess- ment at the next meeting. He should have had this matter completed, but he found that it would be neces- sary to go over the whole of the work, as he was informed by Mr. Houghton that some portion of the manufacture was not being proceeded with, and that some detached buildings were out of use. These he had still to personally examine. The report was adopted. •• LINSEED COMPOUND" for Coughs and Colds . allays irritation. Gives immediate rellef.—Advt.
