THE EX AIN Ell.

MAY 12th 1899.

LET no man lay the The Future of England. flatterine• unction to Hs soul that the E igland of a century hence will be at all like the E 'gland of to-day.. How much different it will be we cannot say, but it is beset with the strong tendency to change in two ways—by the failure of raw materi- als and the transformation of agricultural life. For example, if our coal ware to give out or tc double in price, arid cotton were to be no loager abundant enough to Coale here for sale, a very striking change would occur quickly. Some Lan- cashire towns would cease to exist or contract to villages. Large mining districts would be deser- ted. Manchester and Liverpool would suffer vis- ible decay. Districts where steel and iron indus- tries are now thriving would soon feel the effect of a rise in the price and a fall in the abundance of coal. If American • iron and steal were fairly: low; in price the change would be all the greater. The .cities woald then be sending forth their tsUrplus people to cultivate the soil. Agriculture would become a favourite pursuit. The second great tendency has been manifest for about two veer ttions, and it will soon, we think, reach its cul ninating point. The exodus from the rural districts into the towns has been due to the introduction of machinery in farming. to the better education given in the villages, to the career a towa offers to the you ng of either sex, and to the lack of soeial interest and freedom in the villages. Pr ebably the fall in the prices of agricultural pr educe hs 11 L.l something, but not much, to do with the dapeoulation of agricultural districts. Now, the competition of new countries with old usually begins first in agriculture, and then extends to manufactures. American competition is exhibiting this shifting tendency. Its wheat zone is being pushed further west. Its stocks of cattle for feeding are being de- pleted, and it has to import store stock from Canada and elsewhere. The time cannot be very remote when American wheat will contract as an export. Canadian and Argentine wheat may keep up, and perhaps Russian and Indian. Bat home agricultural products will improve in price accerd- ingly,as foreign cempetition affects oar na anufac tar- ing energy. But it will be said that if our cities are contractinl when our agricultural prices are im- proving, prices must so ea fall again. Teat is so. The probability is, however, that lessened surp!us stocks of food elsewhere rn ay c Impel prices to rise in this country to a point much in advance of what they are now. There will be more labour available for the land, and there will be more land under' cultivation instead of less, because it will be one of the principal occupations of the time. The outlook is not pleasant, but we cannot do batter than familiarise the mind with what is likely to be the outcome of present tendencies. On the side of manufactures, as well as of agriculture, they cannot be described as pleasant tendencies. Yet they can b 3 combated by coal-saving devices and by making rural life more attractive. Tnere is much to be done in either direction. There may be some absolute preventives discovered. Coal was a substitute for wood. What is going to be the substitute for coal 9 S eclat attractions draw into the towns. Why do they not draw into the country 9

THE anxiety of the Government " Further lezislation will Le required." and of the High C mrch party to push back legislation for the better regmlat ion of the discipline and practices of he Church, though it is perfectly intelligible, is not above suspicion. It is hoped that the existing condition of the mind of the country will pass away. It is intended to recover a kind of episcopal authority which shall take away from Parliament and the courts the supra macy fcr the due and effective administration of the law almost as com- pletely as if there had been a bill passed to substi- tute purely ecclesiastical for lay courts. The two Archbishops are now sitting to dote mine questions respecting incense, which are purely legal matters It is not for them to mtk3 the law, which has ,been already declared, and which the clergy in- riminated decline to obey. Their decision will have none of the validity of law, and probably it will not be respected. The hope is, of course,that clergymen who have been unyielding to their Bis- hops will readily obey their Archbishops. Is it not an assumption that goes beyond the known facts? Is not the whole thing an attempt to make the law more binding by giving it the sanction of tlAe Archbishops? How is it, it is sure to be asked, that this support was not instantly given, without the necessity of an inquiry like that which is now proceeding at Lambeth Palace? When we talk about lawlessness in the Church of England it is said that we have assumed too much. The con- trary is the fact. Incense has been declared to be illegal, and two Bishops have so maintained it to be; and their ruling has been set aside. By way of making the whole thing more absurd, we have the two Archbishops solemnly sitting in judgment on the decision of a

===== 0002601_18990512_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER. FRIDAY MAT 12, 1899. RUNCORN AND WIDNE3.

lay court which has already declared incense to be illegal. Is not this something in the nature of a display of lawlessness? The general condition of the Church is marked by several displays of this kind of uncerttinty as to its constitution. The interference of Parliarnem is resented, as if the Church were absolutely independent of the State, and the mood of High Churchmen jest now is that great harm will be dons if, in the name and for the sake of Protestantism, the State should make any fresh enactments and deal at all- with the defiance of Lord HALIFAX and his friends. We have little faith in the Bishops restoring the broken discipline, for the simple reason that its existence is the direct result of their own inattention, on the one hand, and their sympathy with the anti-Protestant movement, on the other. The Bishops have al- lowed the present condition of things to come about, and now we are asked to leave the restora- tion to a hotter state of things entirely in their hands. We have no confidence in them, and there is nothing to be gained by concealing what is the general opinion. We believe, as Sir B. WEBSTER'S resolution of Tuesday affirm ed, in cer- tain eventualities, that "further legislation will be required to maintain the observance of the exist- ing laws of Church and realm." We even go further, because wa hold that the existing laws are insufficient. If a clergyman will not obey the law, he should be deprived of his living, and sent about his business. He should be treated no better and 1!, no worse than a soldier or a sailor.

The London Committee of the International Peace Crusade has sot an example to the world, which we sincerely hope will not be copied. It has been hard at work and at last evolved an ela- borate scheme, in thirty-two articles, for the solu- tion of the universal peace problem, which scheme it has sent to the representatives of the powers in order," that it may prove of some service in assist- ing them." The contribution is one which in all pro- bability,the Committee of the International Peace Crusade will not be very heartily thanked for. Our ambassadors to the Court of Peace, and those of other countries too, will be in no mood for studying the suggestions made to them by people whose only recommendation to their notice is that of being actuated by good intentions. If an example of this kind is copied to even a small extent, the delegates, who have such important business in hand, will be deluged with suggestions for which they can have little or no use. Until the conference is met they will not know what they will be called upon to discuss,and seeing how carefully and judiciously they have been selected they might be left to themselves to make a satis- factory bargain for the benefit of humanity. Peo- ple with suggestions had better save their time, trouble, and paper, and by not worrying their re- presentatives to death, leave them better fitted to cope with the delicate diplomacy and onerous I, duties which lie before them.

Lord Wolseley's speech in regard to the diffi- culty of providing soldiers has been generally com- mented upon, but it really involves no new depar- ture. The Commande,r-in-Chief is a conscriptionist at heart, not merely as a military aul-hority, but as a statesman who believes that compulsory train- ing would be for the physical good of the nation. ae recognizes, however, that the country is against him on this point, and as it is necessary to keep up our complement of soldiers, he urgss that employ- ment should be found for them on their return more sedulously than is at present the ease. It is a pitiable sight to see members of the Corps de Commissionaires, with breasts covered with medals of heroic action, employed in their declining days touching their hats at carriage doors and saluting decadent West Eudrs at michight dances. But old soldiers would be very glad if they could all obtain as good work, and the difficulty that manv of them find is to secure any employment at all. It is the desperate nature of the quest that fre- quently leads them into drinking habits. In their adversity there are plenty of friends willing to treat them to refreshment of this kind, and in their prosperity—that is, when their allowance comes in—they quickly expend it in returning the compliment.

There are some points in the annual report of the Warrington -Medical (nicer of Health, upon which the inhabitants generally can congratu- late themselves, but there are others which call for grave consideration and prompt action. One of the most satisfactory features la the re- port is the extremely low d-ath rate of 17.6 per thousand of the population. This is con- siderably below that of neighbouring towns, and when it is taken into consideration that Warrington is almost wholly given up to manufactures with their attendant dangers, the state of the health of the town is creditable to all concerned. The death rate in such health resorts as Croydon, Hastings and Southport exceeds 14 per thousand, so that it cannot be said that War- it-44ton is very far behind. One of the most serious items in the death rate returns is the infantile mortality. No fewer than 399 children under one year died in the Borough in 1898. This is a slight decrease as compared with the previous year. but it is still above the average for tho United Kinffdom. The birth rate is very high in Warrington. In England and Wales there has been a noticeable decrease in the birth rate in recent years and in 1898 the average was 29.4, which was lower than any other year on record. This statement however,does not apply to Warrington where the average for the past ten years has been 38.9. In a table showing comparative statistics with thirty-three other towns there is only one town with a higher birth rate than Wallington, and that is St. Helens.

Dr. Gornall considers that the time has arrived when an increase of the inspectorial staff should be seriously considered. This will be news to the general body of ratepayers, and especially those who complain that there are too many Corpora- tion officials already. If it is found that another inspector is required perhaps the Health Commit- tee will take into consideration the anlvisability of appointing a lady inspector. Such a near neighbour as St. Helens has appointed one , and with very good results. The Medical Officer again complains of the slow progress made in the paving of streets and back passages. Seven closely printed pages of the report contain the names of unpaved streets and passages in the town, and with the present staff at work it will take at least t wanty years before this necessary work of paving is accomplished. The need of a public abattoir is also referred to, but perhaps the gravest warning is that with reference to hos- pital provision. Di. Gornall points out that in face of repeated warnings nothing has yet been done towards providin2 a small-pox isolation hospital. This statement, if we are not mistaken, is scarcely correct. , ,A. site for the pro- posed hospital has been put chased, and plans for a hospital at an estimated cost of several thousand pounds were passed by the Council at the Febru- ary meeting in 1897.

tBY A CORRESPONDENT.]

At the meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday, the Medical Officer (Dr. 3. Adams) in a report uppn diphtheria in Hilton gives an account of property at Bate's Rtidge which must be in a terrible condition. After applying his ability to describe the ill condition of the place the doctor winds up by saying the "houses are un- lit for human habitation." This is a condition of affairs to be deplored and it is to be hoped that for the l!onour of Halton such remedies may be applied and steps taken as to prevent any such complaint having cause for publication again.

Other subjects of interest dealt with by the Council were Halsby Water and Sewerage ques- tions. The pollution of streams and brooks with sewerage from farms an I private house's: and some amusement was cause I by Admiral Parry's remarks upon the discourtesy of the Thelwall Parish Coun- cil towards the Rural District Council. At the Board of Guardians meeting there was no business of more than ordinary iinportance.

The few ladies—and alas they are very few— who make use of the room allotted to them in the Public Library,complain very much of the pokiness and insufficiency of the accommodation. In the words of one of them" It is a p3ky little place, with just worn for two; and there's a terror of a draught." Whilst sympathising with the ladies I would ask them to bear with the Library Committee for a while, for I feel sure the Committee have known the room is not an ideal one for ladies and intend finding them a better one in the future.

In striking contrast to the alarmist report which appeared some days ago in a Liverpool daily, I no- tice that the same paper gives utterance to a more ,Iparnistic a-pect of tle trade ani prospects of Runcorn. This time I have it on reliable authority that the report emanates from a well informed source and can 133 relied upon. Further I can state on the same authority that the works contemplated are likely to commenc3 in a very short time— possibly a few days.

A SAILOR'S BODY FOUND IN TM OANAL, About noon on Wednesday the body of a sailor, Richard Evans, aged 54, of Aloelfre, Anglesey, who has been reported missing since the 30t.h alt, was found floating in the Runcorn and Westnn Canal at Weston Point. Evans was the mate of the ketch "Sarah Lloyd "of Bangor, and on the previous day to the one he was reported missing visited a friend on the steamship Avon. He left toat ship at about 11 o'clock quite sober, and how he got, into the canal is as yet a mystery. The matter has been reported to the police,and the body now lies at the Weaver Hotel awaiting the coroner's inquiry,which will be held to-clay.

A. NEV LIMN PRY FOR RITNCORY.

Another 9atisracory indication that the com- mercial condition of Runcorn is not in that moribund suite which certain lugubrious reports would lead one to believe is furnished in the fact that arrangements nave just been completed for the purchase of the old gm; works, at the Sprineh, and their immediate claversion into a centre for the manufacture of quicksilver. LIVERMORE BROS.' eMCITS. There is a strong attraction at the Circus this week where Messrs. Livermore Bros. give an enter- tainment in which the Colibris Midgets take a prominent part. BEFORE the MAGISTRATES.

A ROWDY MILITIAMAN SENT TO PRISON. At the Worrington B4rough Police Court on Friday before the Mayor (chairman)and other magistrates,lohn Hooker, militiaman, Orford Barracks, was charged with assaulting John Magrath the previous night and with assaulting P.C.'s Baxendale and Howard in the execu- tion of their duty and further with assaulting P.C. Cun- liffe.—John Magrath,newsboy.ga'd he was selling papers and prisoner came up to him and asked him for a paper Witness gave him a paper and asked for the i but prisoner walked off. Witness told P.C. Baxendale and saw prisoner strike the latter and kick him on the shins. —P.C. Baxendale said the prisoner ws very violent,and William Travis then came to his assistance and rendered very great help. Prisoner also assaulted the young man and kicked him.—William Travis, butcher, of 103.1 Sharp-street, said at a quarter to ten on Thursday night he saw a commotion opposite Parr's Bank in Winwick- street and went to help the policeman. Prieoner kicked him. Witness put him on his back till P.C. Howard came.—P.C. Howard said he moved prisoner from Market Gate at 955 the same night. He was talking to some women and using foul language. Witness told him to go home and wash his mouth out. He went to the assistance of Constable Bazendale later. Prisoner was very rough on the way to the Police Staticn, and kicked him in the stomach.— P.C. Cunliffe paid prisoner as like a inviman when brought to the Police Station.—P.isoner said he had had drink, and as he had been in India he was not respon- sible for what he did when in drink.—The Chief Con- stable said the prisoner told him he had been in the army for 7 years. and had lately enlisted for another period.—The Chairman said the Bench had decided to send prisoner to gaol for one month. He was expressing the feelings of the Bench when he said they were very much obliged for the service rendered by William Travis, and if he wonli accept it they would grant him ss. out Of the poor 13,x. MINOR CASES.

WARRINGTON BOROUIII POLICE COURT. FRlDAY,—Before the lqayor (in the chair), Alderman Roberts, C nmcillor Smethurst, and Mr. James Hepherd. A HUSBAND'S PERSISTENT ORUELTY.—Tnomas Brown, 22, Brick-street. was charged with persistent cruelty towards his wife, Ellen Brown, 4, Victoria street, on the 3rd April.— Mr. Jenkins represented the defendant, and Mr. A.. Browne, who appeared for the complainant, said if the case was proved they applied for a judicial separation. They were married in April 1895. and there were two children by the marriage The defendant was a labourer and his wages were 19s 6d per week. Tha cruelty the man had committed from time to time was something horrible. Defendant had a habit of getting drunk on a Saturday night. In conse- quence she had had to leave the house and sleep in the closet. The wife was a weaver and in consequence of her condition would not be able to work for some weeks.—Complainant gave corroborative evidence. Evidence of the cruelty written by Complainant on a paper was handed to the Bench.—By Mr. Jenkins : She never took up a stool to strike her hus- band whilst he was putting her mother out. She had not taken all the things out of the house.— Defendant said it was quit true he had been summoned by his wife. His mother-in-law was constantly in the house Interfering with him. She was the whole cause of the mis- chief. The house had been stripped of everything. He had BO clothing to lay on at night.—By Mr. Brown: He had been summoned in the Court four times.—The Bench granted a separation order, the complainant to have custody of the children and 3s 6d per week. WoMEN'S QUARREM—Blizabeth Hill was charged with Ming threats towards Martha Stevenson on the 28th April and , Martha Stevenson was charged with assaulting Blizabeth Hill, —Mr. Jenkins who appeared for Stevenson, said his &Pent kept a stall in the `market. He had written a letter to Hill, from what Mrs. Stevenson had said, and it had caused dis- agreement, and Ms. Hill had threatened to strike her. Evidence was given by several witnesses for both sides arbl the magistrates bound both defendants over for six months in one surety of £5, each to pay their own costs. MONDAY.—Before Mr. S. _Wright (chairman), Alderman

Blackhurst and Councillor Smethurst. A CAB DRIVER HEAVILY FlNED.—Samuel Bell, cabdriver. 38. CRUELTY T 3 A BOAT HORaE —John Budd,lll, Manchester road, Droylesden. was summoned for woreing a horse Whilst in an unfit state at Runcorn, on the 25th of April.— Police Constable Harry Fry said he was on duty in Halton-road, Runcorn, at about 11 am., on the 25th of last month. Whilst there he saw the defendant come alone with a horse which was drawing two laden boats going in the direction of Manchester. Noticing that the horse appeared in great pain, he called out to the defendant, and went and examined the animal. Ile found both of its knees had been broken, and that matter and blood was oozing out of the wounds. On questioning the defendant about the condition of the Izerse,he said he hid had it in the stable for the last three weeks. and thought it would do it good to take it to Manchester: He caused the animal to be taken to tde Police Station where it was examined by three other officers. The horse had a very severe wound on the off leg and matter was coming from it. It was in his opinion unfit for the work it was engaged in.—Cross-exam- ined by the defendant: There was both blood and matter coming from the wounds; he should also like to observe that the defendant had covered the wounds over with some black stuff, said to be a mixtnre of lard and burnt co k, so that the wounds would not be noticed by anyone —Pelee Constable Carrington said he had been a constable for the past 17 y sars and during most of that time had the care of the Superin- tendent's horses. He corroborated the last witness. The horse had a large crack across one ~f its knees which opened every time the animal bent its leg. The animal was in such a condition as not to be fit for work.—The defendant who admitted the horse had broke both its knees about three weeks previously, was fined 2s 61 and lOs 61 costs. A SAILING RACE AT HOWLEY.

The first race ot the season under the flag of the Warrington Sailing Club took: place on Saturday on the club course at Howley Quay, the course being three times round. The starters were Main, Messrs. Chappell and Garnett ; Penitent, Messrs. Jeflerson, and Saint, Messrs. Stringer and Whittle. The boats were despatched at 3.35 in a light S.E. wind, by Messrs. Brimelow and Rutu-r. Tne Saiat was the first over the lins,almost on gunfire closely followed by the Penitent, both being well to windward,Maia being last over. It was a dead peg to windward to the Manor Lock, at which place the Saint got hung in irons, and the Maw passing the Penitent. took the lead, closely followed by the other two There was a little alteration of pos:tion now and again between the two last, the Main keeping the lead until 200 yards from the winning post, where she got on a patch and lost all Chance of a prizeo he Penitent just getting home in front of the Saint by eight secs., after a most enjoyable and exciting race. The official finishing times were as follow : Penitent, 4 56 5; Saint, 4-56-13; Main, 4 5745. The Medical Profession recommend the moderate use of well rnaturFd PORT WINE in cases of Iniuenza. WARRINGTON.

LIBERAL SOCIAL MEETING.

There was a pleasant social gathering of the Whitecross and St. Austin's War I Committees on Saturday evening, in the Co-ope rative Hall, Sankey- street. An excellent tea was provided, and the catering of Mrs Hayward gave entire satisfaction. The subsequent proceedings were presided over by Mr. Alderman Roberts. He was supported on the platform by Mr. A. 11. Crosfield, Mr. F. Monks, J P., Councillors Pierpoint, Monks, Plumpton, Evans, Tinnion, Burton, F. R. Roberts, and Mrs. Clay, the representative of Whitecross Ward on the Board of Guardians. A number of interesting and encourag- ing speeches were delivered, and they were agree- ably interspersed by vocal and other items. Solos were efficiently sung by Mr. Arthur Griffiths, Mr. Ireland, and Mr. Davies, and Mr. W. H. Payton, NI us. Bac„ played the pianoforte occo mpanitnents. Ntr. M. Butterfield highly diverted the audienceilwith a Lancashire recitation. A vote of thanks to the chairman concluded a very enjoyable evening. ARMY' AND VOLUNTEER CYCLING CORPS.

In ;;;po-nse to an invitation from the Cycling Club ot the Bth and 40th Regimental District, the members of the cycling corps of the Ist Volunteer Battalion Prince of Wales South Lancashire Regi- ment,, and the 2nd and 3rd Volunteer Battalion Liverpool Reginaent (Liverpool and Southport), took part in a most enjoyable cycling run, on Satur- day afternoon, The Warrington Volunteer cyclists met the Bth and 49th men at, Orford Barracks at 3-15 and rode from there to Crouton, where they met the members of the Liverpool Regiment. Light refreshments were served, and the three sections numbering about 70, then rode to Orford Barracks, where an excellent tea was provided in the library. Mr. Craytherne's catering gave every satisfaction. At eight o'cloek the cyclists rode out to the Com- mercial Hotel at, Rainhill, where a smoking concert was held, and an enjoyable evening spent. Sergeant Major Delmage presided. The whole of the arrange- ments were most successfully- carried out by Quartermaster-Sergeant Burke (Bth and 40th). TFIE BAPTIST UNION TWENTIETH CENTITRY FUSD.

Special sermons were preached on Sunday in con- neetion with this fund. In the morning at Rill Cliff by the Rey. J. S. Hughes, and in the evening at Golborne-street. by the Rev. J. T.iSchofi.eld. The fund was unanimously adopted by the Baptist Union assembly which met in L)ndon in April. It ,is proposed to raise from a quarter to half a million pounds during the next two years. The greater part of the money is to be spent in "the Evangeliz- ation of our country and church extension in the metropoliq, in large towns and cities, and in smeller towns and villages where Baptists are not adequately represented, and where the religious need iq not met by other Evangelical Churches." Over .£.32,000 has already been promised by some of the leading men of the denomination, and the hearty manner in which the fund is being taken up by the churches throughout the whole country gives promise that the full amount will be realized.

PROPOSED CENTRAL UNION OFFICES.

In accordance with a notice given at the previouS meeting of the Board, Mr. PILLING moved that a com- mittee be appointed to report upon a suitable site for central union offices and Board and committee rooms. He mid he had not made up his mind as to the gentle- men who should be appointed members of the committee nor did he know what the expense would be in the matter of office rents, pay stations,and relieving officerl stations. ALL the committee would have to do would he to report upon a site to the Board at a.subsequent meete. ing, and then they could go into the question of cost. Mr. THORNTON seconded the motion. Mr. HICICMAN : When was the last committee of tbig kind disbanded ?

The CHAIRMAN : It never was disbanded. It simply fell to the ground. The Rev. NV. H. DRUMMOND asked if the resolution practically meant the adoption of a policy which would involve the holding of the meetings of the Board away from the Workhouse ? If so it s2emt-d to him that watt an undesirable policy except when the Union was in an, inconvenient position in the country. Bot that was not the case in Warrington. Many advantages were to be derived by meeting people on the Spot, especially if they wanted to interview any of the officers. The more they were in touch with the Workhouse proper, the better. Several c:lmmittees must necessarily meet at the Work- hous, and he did not see any good either in building offices on an elaborate scale as they possibly would beg and a Boardroom away from the Workhouse, especially seeing they had such a large amount of land at the Workhouse. He shr3u:•:l therefore vote against the resolu- tion because he did not believe in the policy on which it was based.

Mr. WILLIAM HOrGEITON thought the Board ought to con•ider the vast amount of money they were spendinZ on the new infirmary. In his opinic.l3 it would be wise to see one project finished before they commenced another one. It would be better to let the matter lit over for another twelve months. _ _

The Rev. J. H. RIDDETTE said he had had a convene tion with Mr. Pilling, and from what he stated, even if the resolution wore carried,the Board would not be cow' mitted to the principle of erecting public offices. He should like it to be an instruction to the committee to make a sort ofprovisiond report at an e.trly date, with.' out any particular site in mind. In the report it could be stated approximately, what would be the probable cost, and the charge lor interest and repayment of capi• tal, as compared with the present rent charges. 'lino doubtecily, the offices now in use were not a credit to Warrington or tho Warrington Union, and the work had to be carried on under severe and unpleasant conditions. He was only anxious that they should make haste slowly in this matter. Mr. NORSHAVV thought it would bP bett,r if they bad a site near the Workhouse. He did not agree wit!) spending money on a now site. Mr. PILLING said thP Board did not pledge itself to anything by passing the resolution. If the committee could show the Board that by taking a site and erectins their own offices it would be advantageous to the Male) it would be well to consider the matter. lie thought there would be a slight increase upon what they were paying row for the rent of offices and stations but Pi course of time the property would belong to the Guar. dians.

Mr. THORNTON said he seconded the proposition be' cause it did not commit the Board to anything. The resolntion was carried by a majority. The f,?l' lowing were appointed on the cornrnitte‘- :---The Chair' man, Vice-Chairman, Mr. George Pilling, Mr. Ll.Thorntoes and Mr. William Flouf.lntori. This concluded the business.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Ameetinq of this Board was held in the Board-room at the Workhouse on Wednesday morninu. Mr. A J Jolley presided, and there were also present :—Mrs Clay, Mrs Edelsten, Bev W fl Drummond, Revs J H Riddette, T Matthews, Dr Patterion, Messrs T H Ashcroft, William Hickman, P Cunliffe, A Jolley, R Forshaw, S Davies, T B Malkin, J Pillinu, H Thornton, H Hihhert, T Halton, Di.nurn, A Brooks, J Johnson, with Mr J C Sutton (clerk), Mr S Sutton (deputy clerk), Mr Ward (work- house master), Mr Wright (superintendent Padgate Schools). THE MASTER AND SUPERINTENDENT'S REPORT. Mr. WARD (workhouse master), reported that Colonel Ridgway had for Fardeu a quantity of flowers. Mr W Moorsorn, Local Government Board InspeAor, visited and examined all parts of the house and also inspected the hooks,—Mr. Wright (superintendent of Padgate Schools), reported the receipt of a box of orang2s from Mr. H. Thornton. SCHOOLS COMMITTEE. _

Mr. A. J. Jolley was -elected Chairman and Mr. Pilling vice-chairman of this Committee. It was decided that no more boys should be sent out of the Schools until after Walking Day. The folowing gentlemen were appointed on the Building sub-committee :—Messm William Houghton, J. Jolley, and R. Forshaw. A letter was read from the Local Government Board, and in reply to the inquiry contained therein, it was resolved that the Scheols staff be not at present increased. The REC. W. H. DRUMMOND asked whether the lettn referred to from the Local Government Board ought not to be put on the minutes. The answer had no meaning apart from the recommendation of the Local Govern- meat Board. He could not for a moment agree with his colleagues in the step; they took with reference to the staff of the Schools. The Crum:ware said the paragraph in the communica- tion from the Local Government Board referred to by Mr. Drummond read :—" I am t 3 add that the Poor Law School Inspector reports that the number of girls under the instruction of Miss Fiasell is too many, and I am to inquire what action the Guardians propose to take with a view of providing her with assistance." Con- tinuing, the Chairman said if they went by the revieed code the Board had a staff for 170 scholars. The attend- ance last year was 165, and under the circumstances the Committee thought it inadvisable to insrease the staff seeing that they were within the limits of the education regulations. The Rev. W. H. DRUMMOND, in answer to the Chair- man, said he did not wish to move an amendment but it ' seemed to him that the minute did no*,: properly record the proceedings, and was quite unintelliaent without the recommendation from the Local Government Board. Mr. PILLING said the committee came to the decision it did simply to find out what the Local Government Board intended to do. The CLERK said the Board must remember that the Local Government Board had the allowances at their discretion, and they might come to the conclusion that the Blari net having attended t 3 their recommendation were not enti'led to be repaid for the schoolmistress. He would suggest that a reply be sent to the Local Govern- ment Board that consideration would be given to their re.cemmenlation and in the event of nece;sity the Board would take some course with regard to it. The minutes were passed, and'tne wording of the re- ply to the Local Government Board was left in the hands of the (Jerk. HOUSE COMMITTEE. At a special meeting of this committee on Friday, April 28th, Mr. Ward temporarily in the chair, Mr. A. J. Jolley was elected chairman for the ensuing year and Mr. Hickman, vice-chairman. The names of four ladies desirous of acting as workers under the Brabazon Em- ployment scheme were submitted and approved, viz., Mi5S Birtles, Miss May, and the Misses Sloane. Nurse Earp appeared before the committee, her trstimoeials being satisfactory, it was recommended that she be ap- pointed at a salary of £27 109. per annum and uniform to coramence duty about the end of June, her salary to be 30 at the en el of twelve months service. WARRINGTON TE YIPER- ANCE FEDERA f lON.

THE ANN UAL REPO UT.

At the florsemarket-•treet Cocoa Rooms on Thursday last week, the ennusl meeting of the War- rington Temperance Federation mi., held. Council- lor Ellison presided. Let ,ers of apology for non- attendance were read from the Rev. J. J. Langnam (president) who was unable to he present in conse- quence of illnes9 and the Rev. J. Yonge till the 1-Gev. F. E. Powell. Mr. WILLIAM ROBERTS, oae of the hon. secre an s, submitted the annual report for 1898. It aa es met the work of the federation for the past year has beer' carried on with a considerable amount of success. After referring to the Sunday night lantern lectures, the re- port continues: " The &ousel Breweter Sessions were as usual full of interest, particularly so because of the ap- plication te transfer the Feathers license trom Bridee-st. to Orford-lane,within a few yards of the site upon which a large new hotel had just been erected. The applica- tion was refused by a majority., and although again ap plied for at the adjourne i Sesaione, and, we regret to say, backed up by the Corporation, the magistrates, we are pleased to say, a sec and time refused to sanction the transfer. We greatly prefer that tins license should be allowed to lapse and strongly recommend the Town Council to take this course. We regret that the application for a full license in Howley was grantee, although vigor- ously opposed. Your cornmmitte wouid call the attention o; our oorough magistrates to the resolution unanimously passed by them requesting the Cnief Constable to bring under their not.ce any publican known to supply drink to children under 13 years of age. Till 9 is being flag- rantly disregarded daily and yet nothing is done. No publicans are mentioned for this offence. Upon whom does the responsibility rest ? We are strongly of opinion that so important a resolutioe should not be allowed to remain practically a dead le'oer. Your cemmittee is also of opinion that the statistics contained in the annual report of the Chief Conatable relating to the arrests for drunkenness do not adequately describe the extent of the evil which undoub:edly exists amongst us. It is an admitted fact that a pers in drunk in our public streets is not interfered with by the police, unless aim- orderly and incapable. If the unhappy individual will only get out of the way,or allow a friend to take charge of him, he is permitted to escape, and thus it is that nundreds of offenders against the law are Snot included in the police atatisties. ialis one remarkable facs can- not pass unnoticed that whilst so many are actually corrected for being " Drunk" it rarely happens that a publican is summoned for permitting drunkennsss. How grossly inconsistent. A word to the wise should be sufficient. Your committeu rejoices that during the pas., year the Warrington Band of Hope Union has again been started, thanks to the successful Bank Holiday Demon- stration organised by this Federatioe in celebration of the Diamond Jubilee. We congratulate the committee most heartily upon having secured as president that de- voted and loyal supporter of all Band of Hope efforts, Mr. K. A. Naylor, of Tnelwall. We sincerely hope that under his able leadership and backed up by energetic officials and a strong committee, the work of the Union will increasingly prlaper among.t 45, Your gMes- raittee recognising the Special eifurtd being made throughout the country on behalf of the Sunday Closing of public-hous:s determined to hold a Sißlikr demonstra- tion in Parr Hall. Through he ;Harts ot our President, I a good deputation was app meted for the W4rrington I meeting, which proveu to be a magolificeat suceeeri. We are also pleased to recogniao the efforts of our sisters in the local branch of the British Women's Temperance Association. Your Committee is greetly interested in the work the ladies have in h ,nd and will gladly render every wsistance it is possible for them to give. Year Committee would again express its warm sympathy with the work of the Police Court Mission, so earnestly and faithfully carried on by our friend and colleague, Mr. H. Taylor, and as a special expression of approval has given a subseription in aid of the funds of the Mid- aims, which so richly deserves the support of our fellow townsmen. In conclusion your Committee recognises with the utmoit satisfaction the increased efforts being put forth by the various local societies, and would impress upon all of them the absolute necessity of unity. We cannot hope to agree upon all minor matters, but the need for greater effort in the good cause of temper- ance impresses us deeply. Warrington may not be a "Degraded Town" although many sincerely believe it is. The fact remains that drinking is lareely prevalent amongst us. The youtheof our town of both sexes fre- quent the Public House in great numbers. Is it becawe no other attractions are provided ? Our wealthy Tem- perance friends should ask themselves this question and seek to answer it. The necessity for a Temperance Hall or a People's Palace is most urgent and pressing and de- mands a solution at our Ennis. We commend this mat- ter to the serious consideration of those who are well able to assist in so desirable an undettaking. Your com- mittee cannot close this report without referring to tbe loss sustained by the removal from Warrington of Mr. J. C. Dalton, one of the secretaries of the Federation. Dur- ing his term of office he rendered most valuable service to the cause, and his removal is deeply regretted."

THE BALANCE SHEET,

Mr. JOSEPH MOUNFIELD, jun. submitted the annual balance sheet which showed a balance in hand of £lO 108 10d. • The year olmmenced with a balance in hand from last year of £9 17s 3d. The subscriptions amounted to £29 lls OJ. The expenses for the Brewster Sessions amounted to £1.2 178 6d. The reports were unanimoualy adopted. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. -

The 11,v. J. J. LANCMAM NV,tS unammcusly re-elected president ; and the vice-preAid-nts were also re-elected. Mr. Joseph Mounfield, jun, was appointed hon treas ;and Messrs. William Roberts and E A Jones, hon secs. The committee is composed of the following : Messrs R Storey, R Taylor,Thotnas Dale, A florrocks, Rowlan is, G S Allen, C H Pigot, S B String-r, E b Jonfs, R Wood, O'Brien, William Nightingale, John Drury, C Nurse, and W Pearson.

THE FEATHERS HOTEL LICENSE. The following resolution was u ianimnisly passed : "That this Federation strongly diapproves of the pr. - p osal of certain members ot the Town Council to rebuild the Feathers Hotel in Bridge-street. and earnestly invites all ministers of religion, members of the Town Council, the various temperance organh-ations and all good citizens to unite in defeating the same." WEST AUSTRALIAN SHARES.

Now that the boom has fairly commenced in the shares of West Australian Gold Mining Compaoies, and Golden Horseshoes are standing at over £3s,L•ilte Views at over £lB, and Ivanhoes at over £ll, and many others at ten times their par value, the public may well devote their attention to some of the lower priced ventures whose properties are well situated for striking the rich lodes contained in the above mentioned mines. From information received, we think the shares of Hannan's Associated Mines, Limited, should be well worth the notice of the speculative investor. This company owns eleven mining leases, with an area of abeut 228 acres, situated on the famous Hannan's Field, Kalgoorlie. The property owned by this company lies only 160 chains to the south of the Great Boulder and Lake View Mines. Mr. W. G. Brookman, the dis- coverer of the Great Boulder, Lake View, Ivanhoe and other suqcossful West Australian mitten, has made a most exhaustive report upon the leases owned by the Hannan's Associated Mines, Limited, an extract from which is as follows : "My conclusion is that you have an extremely valuable combination of leases, which will, I have no doubt, when more systematically opened up and developed prove to contain the Great Boulder Main Lodes, the Ivanhoe Main Lodes, the two parallel lodes to the Great Boulder and Ivanhoe, the Boulder East Main Lode, the reef proved in the Hesperus and Ivanhoe Con- sols Amalgamated, and probably the Lake View Lode. I congratulate you on securing it. Compact areas so near the Boulder and Lake View properties are not readily ob- tained, and I have no hesitation in recommending this property as one which will yield handsome dividends.' The £1 fully-paid shares of the H annsa's Associated Mines, Limited, are now dealt in on the London Stock Exchange at about 21g. per share, and with such good prospects in view aere is room for a substantial rise.

EA.RLESTOWN & NEWTON'.

A CORRECTION.

Last week, in our report of the "Dedication of Memorial Windows at Newton" we stated Oat a window to the memory of the late NUN. J. W. Wat- kins had been given "by the family." We under- stand that this is not quite correct, the window be- ing the gift of personal friends of the deceased lady.

PROPERTY SALE AT EARLESTOWN. At the Wellington Rotel, Sarlestown, on Monday evening before a fair company, the semi-detached residence known as " Plea4ant Villa," Crow-lane, Newton-le-Willows, recently occupied by the late Mrs. Naylor, was brought under tie hammer. Tne house was desc,ibed as cmitaining two sitting roonis,kitchen, scullery, pantry and three bedrooms. front and back garden, contents 580 square yards or thereabouts, ground rent £4 6 I. The bidding was fairly brisk and the propely was knockei down for £250 to Mrs. Flrshaw. EARLESTOWN MISSION.

Special services were held in tile Co-operative Earlestown, on Sunday, in connection with the anniversary of the Sunday School attached to the Earlestown Mission. In the afternoon a service of song entitled" No Surrender," was given by the choir and children, the connective rs °dings being given by Mrs. Francis, of Newton-le-Willows. The service was very well rendered, and was much enjoyed by those present. Mr E Kenyon presided at the organ. In the evening a sermon was delivered by Mr. T. Pringle, of St. Helens. Sacred solos were efficiently rendered by Miss B. Morris and Miss Scott. Collections were made at each service in aid of the School fun is. • GOOD TEMPLARY. The usual weekly meeting of the 1.0.G.T., Hope of Earlestown Lodge was hehl in the Co-operative Hall on Monday night, Bro Wihliama C T, presiding. There was a pod attendance, amongst those pre- sent being visitors from the Samaritan Lodge. Hay- dock and the Great Heart Lodge. St. Helens. The monthly notice of the South-West Lancashire No 1 Convention was read announcing the convention to be held at Atherton on toe 13'h inst. A letter was read from the Great Heart Lodge, St. Helens, giv- ing a hearty invitation to attend the reunion of the Lodge. Two candidates were admitted to mem- bership. Readings were rendered by Bros. Warbur- ton, Cutter, Newman and Francis. The meeting was do-ed as usual. SEttIo.IIS CARRIAGE ACCIDENT AT NEWTON. On Monday morning a carriage accident of an alarming nature happened in South worth-road, Newton. Mr. Peter Thompson ol Lowton, with his two daughters was driving to Earlesrown to busi- ness, when the horse became restive. Mr. Thomp- son managed to control it, but when near the Legh Arms Hotel at Newton it became again alarmed at the noise made by a passing train, and getting be- yond control, it dashed off at full speed. Before it had gone far the conveyance overturned, and all the oces6pants were thrown out and the horse fell. Miss Nellie Thompson was found under the trap badly injured, and Miss Susie Thompson received an incised wound on the head, whilst Mr. Thompson escaped with a slight injury to one of his bands. The trap was smashed,but the horse was uninjured. There-was plenty of assistance at hand, and Miss Thompson was conveyed home, and under medical attentionis progressing satisfactorily.

Mr. H La:Riley, of St. Helens, ap- peared for the relatives of the deceased, Mr. Barlow, certificated manager, appearrd for the firm, and Mr. Matthews,Government inspector of mines,also attended. In reply to Mr. Matthews, a witness said there was no fence round the platform upin which Rigby was work- ing.—The jury retuned a verdict of "Acciden' al death," and recommended that the p'atf irm should be fenced.

A WIFE'S NOSE BROKEN. At the St. Helens Couaty l'atty Sessions on Tuesday, bofore Sir David Gamble, Bart., and Alderman M'Kechnie, James Rotherham, of Baker's-cottages,Bll,i, was charged with an atrium ati d assault on his wife Sarah Ellen. Mr. H. L. Riley defended.—Superintaudent Baxendale, who presecuted, said that the man had be,an remanded for a week on a charge of unlawful wounding but he thought the b2st thing was for the eliarge nsw to be reduced to that of aggravatad assault. On the Satur- day morning in question Mrs. Rotherham came back from the market at Warrineton, and was met by her husband, who was drunk. He began to alms her, and then followed her into the pantry and struck her. He went outside, and when he came in again in a very few minutes, he went up to her and struck her a violent blow on the nose, breaking the bone of the no3e.—ln aeswor to Mr. Riley, Mrs Rolierham said she wanted a separation f cm her husband. There had been a groat deal of trouble owing to drink for some time.—Dr. T. A. Wray, of Penketb. said he was sent for to attend Mrs. Rotherham, ana said her face was shockingly bruisod and swollen, and the bones of the nose fractured. She must have been streck a very severe blow.—Mr. Riley said he could not very well plead a justification for the man's act. There wer,-, however, very extensive cir- cumstances of extenuation. Defendant said that his wiles had constantly irritated and annoyed him by nagging " and unkind remarks. Be pointed out that the man was fairly respectable. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EATtLESTOWN.

(BY A CORRESPONDENT.) FOOTBALL.

Although the football season is over, I cannot refrain from just alluding once again to the Earles- town club, who h ave been compelled from force of circumstances to seek fresh headquarter,. F )t some considerable time they have made the Griffin Hotel their headquarters, but owing to some disagree- ment, they have been ejected. What the diffr- ences are, I cannot say. but they m ere evidently beyond adjustment: 1 do not suppose they will have much difficulty in obtaining other suitable lodgings.

CRICKET.

The weather on Saturday was all that could be desired, and consequently all the local clubs were able to bring off their fixtures. Earlestown met Blackley, and with the debut of the new profes- sional great things were expected, but. the large number cf spectators who assembled were doome I to disappointment,for a poorer exhilition of cricket. by the Barlestown club has never beon seen on the home ground. Of course the professionul cannot be blamed for this, as he was perhaps the best man in the team. The Blackley team completely outclassed the home lot who were beaten by nearly 200 runs. Such a thrashing was entirely due to the wretched fielding. Hu,band missed three catches, and the others were pretty nearly as bad, and if this is to be the sort of cricket the home lot intend to give I fancy the disaster of last season, bids fair to be repeated on a larger scale this season.

The second team were more succeqsful, defeating the second eleven of Blackley by 36 rims, Wain- wright ran up 39 runs and Sanders 34 before they were dismisied. The Earlestown second eleven is a strong combination, and will take a lot of beat- ing.

Newton Ist were at home on Saturday to Stanley who they defeated by 129 runs. Stanley is not the Stanley of old, but still I think they should have made a better show than they did. It was evidently Newton's day out, and they took full advantage of it. Storer was in excellent form with the bat and assisted the score by 72 runs. enarlie flindley was destructive with the ball,capturing 6 of the Stanley wickets for 17 runs.

Newton second team was not so succes,ful as the senior lot, as they succumbed to Stanley 2nd by 42 run 3.

The EarleAtown Primitive Methodists have yet to win their first match. They journeyed to Wigan on Saturday to meet Wigan Methodist free Church but were defeated by 22 rues. Au4tin wiv. again in good form with the ball, capturing 5 wickets for 25 and W. Jones added 19 to the score of 56. Had the others shaped as well, victory would certainly havo rested with the Earlestown lut.

WYCLIFFE " EXCELSIOR " BAZAAR. A SUCCESSFUL VENTUR.E.

The chief attraction in the town this week has unquestionably been the " Bccelsior " Bazar in connection with Wycl:ffe Congregational Caurch. Parr Hall has been the centre of interest and here amid surroundings sugges'ive of an Alpine Village, ladies and gentlemen associated with Wycliffe have been doing their level best to raise sufficient funds to justify them in erecting the prepesed Rigby Memorial Buildings. It would be interesting to trace the progress of Congregationalism in War- rington from its very humble beginnings in 1779 in stepney Chapel,lKing-street. to its present posi- tion, but space forbids. The Rigby Memorial is to provide long-needed rooms for church uses and it is proposed to erect the buildings on the land mediately adjeining the church in Edgeworth- street, and on which some cottage property at present stands. This property eame into the market at the beginning of the present year, and as it had long been regarded as a desire ble and even necessary acquisition, the deacons se- cured it at the auction sale for the sum of £557 103! is now contemplated to demolish the existing cottages and erect a building harmonious in ap- pearance with the architecture of the church,which shall include an assembly room capable of aceotn- modating 300 persons, and be suitable for lectures, week evening services, Congregational meetings, &c. ; also a ladies' parlour, for the use of working and other ladies' meetings, a ves're, &re It is believed the building can be erected for £1,700, but the cost of furnishing will probably bring the total cost to £2,500. By associating the name of Rigby with the buildings, not only will respect be paid to the devoted labours and muniflc-,nt support of the late Mr. and Mrs. Samuel Rigby of ilruche , Hall to Congregationalism in Warrington, but the memorial will serve to remind Warrington people of a former citizan who has added to its material prosperity and who rendered great service in the public weal. When a bazaar was suggested as one means of raising the necessary funds, the project was taken up enthasiaetically, and for many mouths past, ladies have lavished their time, skill and care, and used their influence far and near in providing the articles which were seen in such great profusion on the stalls when the bazaar was opened on Tuesday. The gentlemen too, planned and wrought schemes for its succees, and as the event drew nearer all seemed to live in what may he termed a bazaarifled atmosphere. The decoration of the hall had been entrusted to Messrs. F. Le Maistre and Co., the emi- nent losnar experts, or Manchester, and the trans- formation scene which they eff:icted in the interior of the hall was admired by all who beheld it. We furnished our readers with a description of it in our last iesue,and it is therefore unnecessary to say more,on the present coca-ion than that the Alpine village with all its picturesque surroundings was was faithfully portrayed, and served to justify the selection of the motto "Excelsior" in connection with the bazaar. _ _ _

_ The eff .ctiveness of the scene was heightened by the tasteful display of tke goods upon the various stalls. It would require a huge catalogue to des- cribe the articles off Iced for sale. bloods in every form and colour, shape and shade w -re to be seen. Photographs of the late Mr. Rigby were given prominent places on two stalls, and his genial face and kindly" smile seemed to express approval of the proleedings. Additional brightness was lent to the scene by the gay dresses of the ladies in attend- ance, and this living scene of colour blended most harmoniously with that of the decorative artist, in fact the animate and the inanimate made up a pleasing ensemble.

BUILDING TRADE DISPUTE. The executive of the Lancashire an -I Cheshire Federa- tion of Master Btrlders, yesterday passed a resolution strongly recommending the National Association to order lock-out of 25 per cent. ef unionists in building trades as soon as convenient after their next meeting at Birmingham on the 18tn. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. _- RUGBY FOOTBALL. As all members are wily aware toe annual meeting of the Warrington Football Club will be held in the Work- men's Club on Tuesday evening next. If the agenda paper is anything to go by the meeting will be one of unusual intereLt Never were there so many candidates for the different offices. Members have five gentlemen for prssident to select From, three for secretary, and 25 for cernmittee. It sufficient good men and true can- not he selected from all these to carry on the Club suc- cessfully, then it will bo time to give Rugby football up. 'believe the nominations this year do afford opportunity for several beneficial alterations, and the members will have only themselves to blame if they allow things to go on ie the unsatisfactory manner in which they have dote In the past season. The financial statement, now that we have it before us, does not show the club in such a very black light. llt is true that it is poorer by ROM £3OO than was the case a year ago, and the finances have sunk to a suffi- ciently low level if the club is to be carried on at all,but still there are circumstances which, when considered, briehten tee prospect considerably. The huge outlay (£375), entailed by the compulsory setting back of the fences, turnstiles, offices etc., after the second team ground had hen sold by Messrs Greenall almost entirety accounts for the loss on the year's working. The gate- money tots up very satisfactorily, inasmuch as it ex- ceeds last year's total, but there is no glossing over the fact that since the new ye%r the income of the club has been practically non est. Interest was evaporating rapidly and it w as only the rapid conclusion of the season's en ei gements wlrch saved toe club from greater logs. It is quite apparent that it wily requires skilful manage- ment and enterprise te set the club on a firm founda- ton once more, and for this we must look to the new nfficids who everybody expect will be elected. The formation ot a really class team is the first and primary object. Money will then roll in—l am convinced—and sucli ideal scheme's as the erection of a proper and com- modious pavilion on the ground can be considered fully I hen. I hope there will be a goo- d meeting, largely attended, amicably conducted, and sureltant in those changes wh ch I have always advocated. The suggestion made in this column to reduce the committee to six members in addi'ioo to the hon. officials has. I see, been adopted, and if the members are fully alive to the advantages accruing fie m it it will be confirmed unanimously. I Much can be slid in favour of the other resolutions brought foiward, and it is probable that they will be carried. CR- ICKET. Warrineton had to try conclusions with Leigh on Saturday, and were short of the services of Dr. Smith and E. Connor, two players whom they could ill spare. This match was away and the home team had first innings, Garside and Mayall, two professionals, going to the wickets. The trundling was shared by Thewlis and J. W. Bibby. The professionals did not find the bowling conducive to scoring, and the runs had only got in the t-ens when Garaide received his conga at the hands of Thewlia. G. Clarke succeeded Bibby with the ball and met with early success. Circumstances favoured the battieg better after this and at 60 for 2 wickets W. H. Podmore relieved Thewlis, and the batsmen were obliged to tone down their hitting a hit. Clarke eventually found a weak spot in Mayail's defence who retired with a well played 28 to his credit. In the next over Williams, another professional was dismissed. The score stood at 66 now, for about half the side. One or two other changes were made with the bowling but the score steadily mounted and at 140 for 8 wickets the Leigh , captain decided to close their innings. To save the match War- rington had an hour and a half. The professional and J. Callon took their stand at the wickets. The latter bit out confidently, and Thesslis in strong contrast played cautiously, but was diatrifsed for five runs. A. W. Denham who sucaeeded him was more promising in style and the , partnership proved an advantageous one. Calion's play was justified by his score and he had 33 runs to his accoust when caught out. Caotain Plomer gave some good as-ista.nce to the score, but the rest of the batsmen gave little EISiiBI'aTICO and when the time for wicket , drawing came W. H. Pndmore and Captain Plomer were fairly well set. The Warrington men had then totalled 87 for eight wickets the game being a draw. There is some cause for congratulation in this. The visitors had not a strong team on and the Leigh men were worthy of their steel—or willows. Callon, for Warr ' ington made a dashing display and deserves every credit. Of the home team J. H. Unsworth's score of 34 (net out) was a fine exhibition well seconded by Mayan aho put on 28 runs. G Clarke had the best of the bowling on the Warrington side, but on the other hand the visitors aid not find Garside's trundling prolific of scoring as out of 15 overs, there were 8 maidens,and 3 Warrington wickets were taken at a cost of 6 runs.
